Climate change problem

PEEB Cool is addressing a coexisting mitigation and adaptation problem. Green buildings can reduce vulnerability to climate change by maintaining thermal comfort in buildings that are not mechanically cooled even under future climate conditions, while addressing mitigation needs by reducing energy consumption.

### Adaptation: The IPCC has stated that it is very likely that there will be more frequent hot temperature extremes on most land areas on daily and seasonal timescales, and that it is very likely the frequency of heat waves will increase. In addition to these extreme weather changes, maintaining thermal comfort in buildings with hot climates is expected to present an increasing challenge as temperatures rise due to climate change, and this trend will continue. Increases in heat load and a rising body core temperature are expected to be more pronounced for people who are ill-thriving because of heat stress.

Heat stress also has economic effects as it is projected to reduce GDP per capita worldwide by 2.1 per cent and GDP for US$2,400 billion in 2030.*

### Mitigation: The construction and operation phase of buildings is responsible for 40% of all energy-related CO2 emissions globally, and this share will increase with the energy transition. Due to population growth, rapid urbanization and economic development, final energy demand from buildings is projected to increase by around 65% between now and 2050 compared to 2015 levels.

To stay on track to meet global climate ambitions set forth in the Paris Agreement, the energy intensity per square meter of buildings needs to improve on average by 30% by 2030 (compared to 2015). This means a new-build stock will need to be around 40% more efficient than today. In hot climates, even more challenging in hot climates, as a significant fraction of energy use in buildings is for air conditioning and ventilation, which are typically provided by air conditioning annually equates 2,000 TWh/year that is expected and is likely to peak by 2050.

As long-lived assets, green buildings constructed today reduce vulnerabilities that would otherwise contribute to climate change and increase the resilience of buildings in their lifetimes. There is a need to act now on this market which represents USD 4.5 trillion per year**.

How? By implementing effective (dilactinative) measures to maintain cooler temperatures than in conventional buildings:

- Through installing more efficient cooling systems and using renewable energy to power these systems
- Using low carbon construction materials
- Through policy interventions that enhance public policies incentives and support the building sector industry

With adequate policies and adequate financial incentives. Many countries still do not explicitly mention the building sector in their NDC or do not have mandatory thermal regulations for buildings, for instance.

Proposed intervention: PEEB Cool is aiming to transform the construction sector at scale by supporting resilient and energy efficient building, so called green buildings, design, construction and operation.


*Information and public health advice; heat and Health. World Health Organization (https://www.who.int/healthtopics/publications/heat-and-health/)
** Working together for a sustainable future: The role of the built environment in productivity and decent work – International Labour organisation, 2019
*** UN Environment Programme Global Energy Agency (2017): Towards a zero-emission, efficient, and resilient buildings and construction sector. Global Status Report 2017
**** Global ABC report status for buildings and construction 2019

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V8.0 | PAGE 6

The proposed intervention targets 11 countries in 4 continents with climates in which the temperature reaches level at which heat related risks become significant in Africa (Djibouti, Morocco, Nigeria, Tunisia), South-East Asia (Indonesia, Sri Lanka), South America (Argentina, Costa Rica, Mexico) and Eastern Europe (Albania, North Macedonia).

The selection of the countries was based on a pre identified selection criteria:
- Level of vulnerability to increased temperatures and climate extremes like heat waves
- A preliminary gap assessment for specific needs in the residential, healthcare and commercial sectors.
- Similarity of technical barriers.
- The level of readiness and experience of the AFD

The results of this analysis have been included in the feasibility study in section 2.4.

The proposed intervention has two components - an Investment Facility and an Enabling Facility
- An Investment Facility (component 1), with a budget of 30 MEUR, or an average of 2.75 MEUR per country to invest in green buildings through the transformation of the buildings sector environment towards more buildings through a new framework for building trainings.

The components have been designed to each address key barriers that are preventing the construction industry in the targeted countries from shifting from current unsustainable practices to a low carbon, climate resilient development path. It is based on the unique proposal that the two facilities go hand-in-hand and complement one another. The Investment Facility will provide financing for projects to incentivize and regulate. Thus the Programme will contribute to a paradigm shift in the sector, leading to more sustainable use of building materials, efficient cooling systems and low carbon construction materials.

Climate impacts/benefits

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V8.0 | PAGE 7

A


Promoting green buildings brings a triple win: providing a powerful tool to stimulate investment in the green economy (147k jobs created), improving resilience of the most vulnerable populations as buildings are better able to withstand climate change, and reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.

PEEB Cool investments will improve the socio-economic resilience of all social strata in the communities where they take place, but particularly of low- and middle-income groups, as a result of the construction of improved low- and middle-income housing as well as public buildings providing essential services. The beneficiaries of the PEEB Cool Programme are the users of buildings constructed with the support of the Programme. The total number of direct beneficiaries of the Programme has been estimated at 1.133 million people (approx. 561,373 females).

Climate change impacts come in two forms:
- Direct impacts, attributable to investments in subproject(s) / end-beneficiary projects promoting energy efficiency in buildings, including efficient cooling systems and low carbon materials.
- Indirect impacts, attributable to improved regulatory frameworks including national energy efficiency regulations, construction standards and certification commitments by industry actors.

The total estimated amount of GHG emission reductions induced by the program is 1.563 MTCO2e over a 25-year period (49 EURt CO2 eq for the GCF contribution or 9.85 EURtCO2 grant, based on an estimate of the impact of energy efficiency investments, construction standards and certification only).

<sup>1</sup> *Subproject* in the Funding Proposal refers to transactions from the PEEB Cool facility - potentially investments into a number of buildings. *End-beneficiary projects* in this Funding Proposal refers to individual transactions as part of a credit line subobject

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

I- Climate change problem

Climate change is driving a sharp increase in the global demand for cooling. This demand is primarily driven by population trends and the higher incomes in developing countries, but it is also connected with the climate vulnerability of the populations. Exposure to extreme temperatures and susceptibility to heatwaves are increasing due to climate change. The frequency and intensity of heatwaves have increased, leading to a greater need for improved emergency services. Such impacts are especially threatening to countries which are more susceptible to extreme heatwaves. While addressing the demand for cooling may improve the resilience of health systems in these countries, it will also increase energy consumption and GHG emissions. Increased active cooling leads to higher energy (and coolant) use, and therefore increases greenhouse gas emissions. The PEEB Cool programme is designed to address this challenge by identifying opportunities that will address both mitigation and adaptation needs through the construction of green building, by addressing cooling needs for improved comfort and well-being, while minimizing GHG emissions.

Adaptation needs

The countries of the PEEB Cool Programme are spread over seven different climatic zones: Mediterranean, humid subtropical, tropical, equatorial, and mountain (or high plateau) and continental climates. In each of these climates the temperature reaches levels at which heat-related risks become significant. These risks include increased mortality from heatwaves, increased risk of infectious diseases, and other health risks: more regular and extreme heatwaves and an increase in mean temperature. According to the IPCC's scenario, the mean annual temperature rise for countries in Africa ranges from 3°C to 7°C by the end of this century (IPCC, 2014). The PEEB Cool programme is designed to address these risks.

Country specific climate hazard information (presented in the country forms in the annexes of the feasibility study) indicates that for most countries climate projections indicate that the risk due to high temperatures will increase drastically. According to the World Health Organization, "the room temperature should be kept below 32°C during the day and not exceed 18°C at night" (World Health Organization, 2015). Children under five years of age or have chronic health conditions." Electric fans may provide relief, but when the temperature is above 35°C, may not prevent heat-related illness. Research indicates that, in high-emission scenarios, the number of deaths from heatwaves could increase by up to 40% (IPCC, 2014).

Vulnerability to climate change is connected to increased exposure to high temperatures. The main pathways relevant to PEEB Cool are linked with:

- Direct vulnerability due to increased risk to adverse health effects from high indoor temperature
- Secondary vulnerability due to stresses to the health system from:
    - Increased emergency health services needs, especially during heatwaves
    - Reduced productivity of the health professionals in hospitals

* Information and public health advice: heat and health. World Health Organization.
(https://www.who.int/gho/heat/publications/heat-and-health/en/)
* Copeland, S., et al. (2015). Heat-related excess mortality under climate change scenarios. The Lancet Planetary Health, Volume 1, Issue 9.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 9

## Drivers of vulnerability due to reduced productivity in businesses and schools.

The reduced exposure to high temperatures, through improved indoor thermal comfort, will lessen the vulnerability of the population, minimizing the risks in health, the system, and improving productivity.

Increased indoor temperature has been linked with adverse health effects, reduced productivity, and increased need for emergency services. Higher indoor temperatures have been linked with worsened respiratory symptoms, lower blood pressure, increased insulin intake to patients with type one diabetes, and increased risk of heat stroke.

Primary and secondary health providers are also vulnerable to increased temperatures. Several studies found that heatwaves lead to increased needs in emergency medical care **1**, while others have linked heatwaves to increased hospital admissions for cardiovascular disease.

A series of studies have linked thermal comfort with productivity. Temperatures that are higher than 28°C cause performance loss at work and "neutral" or "slightly cold" conditions have been associated with increased productivity, especially in the case of office workers. Students in all classrooms at all stages of their education, i.e., primary, secondary and at university, report feeling comfortable on the cooler side of the thermal sensation scale, while a number of studies have linked higher temperatures to lower performance.

## Argentina

Argentina is a dense population in urban areas and vulnerable to a variety of natural disasters extreme heat. The INCCO reports that Argentina's 65th subdivision has the highest number on record. Periods are expected to increase. Moreover, the country has experienced the number of days with high temperatures.

### Projections show a change in temperature between 1981-2016 rising by 3.4°C and significantly warmer.
#### The warmer weather.
The average maximum is 15.5°C. However, the country is experiencing significant warming, with temperatures increasing by around 1.7°C since 1965. The number of hot days has increased by 2.8% to a high rise by 3.7% between 1961 and 2014. Moreover, the number of cold nights has fallen, with a reduction in the number of nights of 2.2% and a drop in the number of mild nights of 2.4% since 1985. There is also a clear upward trend over the last 60 years in Costa Rica.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

## Nigeria

Nigeria has seen an average temperature of around 5 to 7°C above normal over the last thirty years (with total warming with a larger increase in the south). The World Bank projects that the number of hot nights will increase between 1986 and 2030, from 40 hot days per year to 73 days now projected.

Dry spells and hot periods are expected to continue to expand. The whole country will experience more warming, with an increase in extreme heat events and populations at risk.

Projections show a maximum temperature of around 1°C by mid-century and reaching up to 2.7°C by the end of the century.

## North Macedonia

Average temperatures have increased by 0.5°C in recent decades, projected to rise to 0.5°C of warming.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## Sri Lanka

Over the last 50 years, temperatures have increased at a rate of about 0.2°C per decade, with greater increases in maximum temperature.

World Bank data shows a mean annual temperature increase of 1.4°C between 1968 and 2010, with a mean rise of 0.3°C for the minimum of 13.5°C. Maximum temperatures in recent decades have increased significantly by an average of about 1.7°C per decade over the last 50 years.

By 2040, Sri Lanka is expected to continue with fewer cold and more hot conditions, leading to a projected increase in energy consumption for cooling days (preliminary).

## Tunisia

World Bank data shows a mean annual temperature increase of 1.4°C between 1968 and 2010, with a mean rise of 0.3°C for the minimum of 13.5°C. Maximum temperatures in recent decades have increased significantly by an average of about 1.7°C per decade over the last 50 years.

By 2040, Tunisia is expected to continue with fewer cold and more hot conditions, leading to a projected increase in energy consumption for cooling days (preliminary).

## Projections

### Sri Lanka
- **Projected change in temperature:** A range of 1.7°C by mid-century, reaching up to 2.5°C by the end of the century.
- **Projected change in cooling degree days:** Projected between 308 and 469 per year (on capital level), with a projected increase from 294 to 359 days for the period.

### Tunisia
- **Projected change in temperature:** A range of 1.7°C by mid-century, reaching up to 2.5°C by the end of the century.
- **Projected change in cooling degree days:** Projected between 308 and 469 per year (on capital level), with a projected increase from 294 to 359 days for the period.

## Adaptation Benefits

### Sri Lanka
- **Improved productivity**
- **Improved thermal comfort in commercial sectors**

When outdoor temperatures are high, it is challenging to maintain thermal comfort in buildings. Due to climate change, buildings which are not mechanically cooled (such as low-income social housing units, waiting areas in healthcare centres or classrooms) will see an increase in indoor temperatures, and possibly an increase in the amount of time that indoor spaces are too hot to be used for their intended purpose.

The International Labour Organization estimates that a 1°C rise in temperature could reduce total working hours worldwide by 2.2 per cent and global GDP by US$2-4 billion on in 2030.

### Mitigation needs

The operation and control of buildings is responsible for 40% of all energy-related CO₂ emissions and consumes 36% of the global final energy. Due to population growth, rapid urbanization

and economic development, final energy demand from buildings is predicted to increase by 50% by 2050 compared with 2015 under business as usual scenarios. To be on track to meet global climate ambitions set forth in the Paris Agreement, the energy intensity per square meter of the global building stock will need to decrease by at least 60%. This can only happen through a combination of mainstreaming of highly energy-efficient new buildings and deep renovation of the existing building stock.

The IEA has analysed building sector energy intensity at the regional level. It has scored different regions on an energy intensity index (all starting with a score of 100 in 2000). Currently, Central and South America scores 96.6; Africa 92.1; Europe 78.5; and Asia 73.1. The IEA’s Sustainable Development Scenario projects that by 2050 the energy intensity index will be 40 in Europe and 35 in Asia, while the energy intensity index is set to fall to 69.4 in Central and South America, 36.1 in Africa, 59.8 in Europe and 39.3 in Asia. The effort to be made if a huge shift to achieve the Paris Agreement targets.

A significant part of GHG emissions from the building sector is due to energy use for cooling. According to the IEA’s Sustainable Development Scenario, global cooling demand will increase by 2050 and in the business as usual scenario this is expected to increase to 6.20 TWh in 2050. CO₂eq emissions generated by space cooling would then almost double, from 1.135 Mt in 2016 to 2.070 Mt in 2050.

In the countries of the programme, GHG emissions from energy consumption in buildings are estimated at 419 million tCO₂eq based on their most recent energy balances (out of total emissions of 1.83 billion tCO₂eq) and an average cooling degree day of 220 (months).

Various indices are used to indicate changes in temperature that affect thermal comfort and the need for mechanical cooling. One widely used index is cooling degree days. Projections for the impact of climate change on this index are described in the table above.

## Baseline and context

The baseline for a specific Subproject describes the expected thermal performance and energy use for cooling for buildings as business as usual design. By incorporating passive measures* to maintain cooler temperatures than conventional buildings, so-called green buildings will decrease the indoor temperature by 1–2°C compared with conventional buildings. The results of the Feasibility Study; green buildings therefore reduce emissions and improve resilience to climate change, particularly in low-income countries where air conditioning or refurbishments are too costly.

The baseline for estimating mitigation benefits is established by assessing the Business as Usual energy performance of a building using the relevant national building code (e.g. ASHRAE 90.1) and using more detailed assumptions if they are available. In countries with an existing and effectively implemented building code, it may be possible to use this baseline for Subprojects that ensure that they take into account the energy performance requirements of the building energy code.

To estimate adaptation benefits and costs, the Business as Usual baseline is produced by using a thermal simulation model that assesses thermal comfort outputs at an hourly timestep. Input data consists in reference buildings' plans (per climate and types), and Energy Plus weather files.

The With Subproject scenario involves:

## Mitigation Subprojects with an average of the two following possibilities (reflecting the programmatic nature of investments in each country that will cover more than just one sub-investment)

- **Typical Investments which result in 20% GHG emissions reduction compared to the baseline; and/or 20% energy consumption savings compared to the baseline; and/or 20% water consumption savings compared to the baseline:** It is a new construction – the actual reductions per building varying according to the specific geography and outputs of the EDGE model.
- **Typical Investments which result in >40% GHG emissions reduction or 40% energy consumption savings compared to the existing situation before renovation if it is a renovation project; and/or >20% water consumption savings per building:** varying according to the specific geography and outputs of the EDGE model.
- **Adaptation Subprojects:**
    - Building occupants (male/female) should experience an improvement in their indoor thermal conditions of at least 20% compared to the baseline or the existing situation.

The current climate conditions were based on climate variables already included in EDGE software for each country. The future climate conditions used in the analysis are based on plausible scenarios were based on a dynamic simulation of building conditions in the coming decades. The future climate conditions were generated by running the EnergyPlus weather file with current conditions through Energy Plus weather files with current conditions to Energy Plus weather files with all A2 IPCC scenario.

This approach allows for flexibility and consistency between the different countries. Details on the context and the expected potential for impact in each country targeted by PEEB Cool is provided in the country forms (annex of the Feasibility Study) and in the section 4.2.4 of the Feasibility Study. The methodology to assess the baseline is described in section 4.3.1.2 of the Feasibility Study.

PEEB Cool will address the need for green buildings in different sub-sectors which include the following:

- **Education facilities:** In Africa a majority of schools are not cooled. Low budget construction with metal roofing is common, as is overcrowding, which further increases the overheating problems. These same countries have not yet reached 100% school enrolment and therefore need to build many more schools.
- **Healthcare:** The technical issues are similar for healthcare centres, with a majority of spaces uncooled. Hospitals, on the other hand, are often at least partially air conditioned.
- **Housing:** While middle-income housing will often have air conditioning, low-cost housing in the target countries is not.

One thermal dynamic simulation in a social housing investment in Latin America for example showed that with low tech and relatively simple solutions thermal comfort could be improved by 20% in single-family housing and between 27% to over 50% in multi-family housing compared to the baseline scenario. Social housing projects are often located in areas where there is a high demand but where cooling energy consumption is low, building design that provides thermal comfort is important for resilience. The cost of these solutions can be relatively low as they do not require any major changes at a low cost that is in keeping with the purpose of the buildings as low-income social housing. The improved case has a cost of 355 USD/m², i.e. an additional cost of 24%, which is justified by the

significant improved indoor conditions, which will benefit the health of the residents and in particular of vulnerable groups such as the elderly.

Green buildings have high impacts thanks to passive measures

The design of the building is critical to improve thermal comfort by passive, non-energy consuming measures (e.g. cooling) including such as (i) properly orienting the building, (ii) reducing the window-to-wall ratio, and (iii) insulating the building.

The Feasibility Study describes a case study of a school without mechanical cooling in a tropical climate. In the baseline building, in the 2020 climate conditions inside were uncomfortable during 72% of the year compared to only 15% for the proposed building. The cost of cooling the baseline building in 2050, while the number of uncomfortable hours rose compared to 2020, the maximum temperature remained under or equal to 33°C. The cost of construction of a baseline classroom was evaluated at €486 (EUR/m²), and the cost of cooling for the same classroom would be at €495 (EUR/m²), bringing the incremental cost to 11% EUR/m², or 31% of the cost of construction.

How to mitigate GHG emissions due to cooling?

A second lever to mitigate GHG emissions due to energy use for cooling is the efficiency of the cooling system itself. The best new equipment can be five times more efficient than old technology that is still in operation today. This will require a coordinated action between policy and market actions on cooling systems, through a suite of policy interventions including, for instance, the use of energy labels.

The third level is making use of renewable energy. This can be achieved both at the building level with PV panels on the roof or the facades, or at the neighbourhood level or in public buildings with the use of a renewable-based mini grid or with the development of a renewable-based district cooling system. The Programme will promote this third level by financing eco-districts and solar auto-production of buildings.

Both levels of solutions when considered in the design of a factory in a sub-tropical climate. It was assessed that integrating COP 6.0 chiller and PV panels would reduce the need for energy by 41.7%.

At construction stage, working on low carbon construction materials is a priority

The raw resource extraction, manufacturing, transportation, usage, and end-of-life stages of building products are responsible for up to 50% of their total lifecycle GHG emissions. GHG emissions (mostly CO2) released before the building begins to be used will be responsible for about half of its total lifecycle emissions. The difference between now and 2050 is that passive measures and more efficient cooling systems, the carbon footprint associated with buildings materials and construction will increase.

Peeb Cool will support the use of low carbon materials where possible, allowing for high emission reduction potential such as bamboo, palm leaves, compressed earth bricks, straw, wood, or bamboo. The Programme will promote those materials in the building design choices and through awareness raising activities.

## Related projects and interventions

PEEB Cool is envisioned as an extension of an on-going PEEB Programme implemented by AITD Group in Sri Lanka and North Macedonia, with a widened geographical scope and additional eligible thematic and innovative tools. However, it embraces PEEB's approach, which is a focus on implementation of green building projects (through investment), whose outcomes are generalised in updated regulations and standards, as well as the development of a Green Building Programme (GBP) as foreseen within the Programme. Finally, the market for low-carbon materials and construction methods will be developed to ensure that PEEB Cool is competing with the large and established markets for conventional construction products and methods.

### B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max 1000 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

Only with a decarbonisation of existing and new buildings and an increase in adaptive capacity will the buildings and construction sector globally be on track to meet climate-resilient development path that is compatible with the goals of the Paris Agreement. This shift must be as rapid as possible.

Buildings are critical to delivering climate change mitigation and adaptation outcome. On the one hand, standard buildings do not allow to cope with future temperature increases and with the expected increase in extreme weather events. On the other hand, energy use for cooling is an increasing fraction of building energy use and related GHG emissions. The Programme addresses these challenges through the construction of buildings with reduced cooling needs by using passive design measures, efficient cooling technologies and low carbon construction materials.

Theory of Change

The Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 19

# The Theory of Change Diagram (Figure 3) presents the integrated approach that this facility adopts to combine investment in green buildings construction and technical assistance.

This describes the goal statement of this programme: If (i) Subproject stakeholders are supported technically, (ii) financial resources for green building projects are mobilized and innovative financing mechanisms are developed; and (iii) actors are enabled and supported, then the climate change resiliency of buildings will be improved. As a result, energy efficiency in buildings will increase, as well as efficient cooling systems and low carbon construction materials in residential, health, commercial, and education buildings will be scaled-up. This is because investments in the green buildings market will be increased; regulatory frameworks will be improved and the capacity of stakeholders will be strengthened.

The outcomes of this programme will include:

- Stimulated investment in the green building market
- Improved resilience for most vulnerable populations as buildings are better adapted to climate change.
- GHG emissions will be reduced from buildings in the residential, education, health and commercial sectors.
- There will be improved regulatory frameworks including national energy efficiency regulations, performance standards and certification commitments by industry actors, as well as training and increased capacities of human resources.
- A contribution to the creation of local green jobs where local employment is promoted in green buildings.

The outputs of the programme have been designed to support project owners in the various phases of implementing a green building. These include capacity development for stakeholders during the operational phase. This includes developing the capacity of stakeholders in the green buildings sector. Investments for green buildings projects will be appraised and financing agreements will be signed with financial institutions. The programme will also provide technical assistance to countries that demonstrate the potential for a green recovery. The programme will support the development of policies, strategies, and action plans on energy efficiency and low carbon construction materials. Training and awareness raising will be conducted to enable private and public sector actors to implement reform policy. Finally, knowledge products will be developed and experiences shared to reinforce the global transformation of green buildings.

# Barriers

There are clear trends in the barriers for the construction of green buildings that exist in the programme countries. These threats underly the conceptual design of the Programme. At the same time, it is important to note that these barriers vary significantly from country to country and within a country. The extent to which specific barriers affect a country is influenced by the existing regulatory frameworks as well as the expectations of a country with energy efficient and resilient buildings.

# Technical barriers: Stakeholders lack technical knowledge and experience to build green buildings

A technical barrier that was most prominent in many of the countries of the list is that limited knowledge, Sector stakeholders (e.g. Project owners, architects, engineers, contractors, and construction companies) lack knowledge and awareness on energy efficient and resilient buildings, including the technical knowledge and experience in planning and implementing energy efficient and resilient buildings.

PEEB Cool addresses the technical barriers by providing technical project assistance to the Subproject owners in partner countries on the feasibility, design and construction of energy efficient

**Page 22**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 20

## Low-carbon and resilient buildings (for instance social housing, schools, and health facilities). This assistance will be provided through two Programme Facilities.

The Investment Facility will conduct feasibility studies for a design with improved thermal performance and energy efficiency in the construction of new or retrofitted buildings in countries participating in the Green Climate Fund (i.e. developing countries such as semi-urbanities or cities, or dormitories) (Activity 1.2.1). The Investment Facility will also assist Subproject implementation through direct supervision and monitoring of the project activities to ensure that the design is implemented according to the approved plan and that the measures are effectively implemented. It will provide on-site capacity development for completed Subprojects to ensure a long carbon operation and sustainability (Activities 1.2.2 and 1.2.3).

The Enabling Facility will support the construction of energy efficient, low-carbon and resilient buildings as well as meeting the building code requirements, and disseminates the acquired knowledge to the wider ecosystem of stakeholders (i.e. developers, planners, builders, and banks) in order to ensure that they are able to implement green buildings. It will provide training on green buildings within countries and internationally to strengthen institutional and regulatory systems for the implementation of green building practices. The Enabling Facility will also support lifetime energy efficient operations, scaling and replacing of the solutions (Activities 2.4.1 and 2.4.2).

## Financial barriers: Building owners are reluctant to cover higher costs of building green buildings

Across all geographies, we observe a strong reluctance of project owners to bear any extra costs associated with improved buildings. This financial barrier has increased in importance as a result of the economic crisis. In particular, in countries where the real estate market has collapsed and participating countries have suffered significant losses and a major drop in growth. This reduces the willingness to engage in green construction programmes, which often have higher upfront costs than conventional building practices.

## Financial barriers: Industry actors and banks are reluctant to adapt their services to support construction of green buildings

Based on international experiences, the Enabling Facility develops sectoral frameworks for the broader transformation of buildings sector actors that ensure competitiveness and reduced risk, which leads to the mobilization of further investments and the development of a local offer (Activity 2.1.1). The Investment Facility will provide technical assistance in the design phase to promote, as international experience shows that industry labels have a powerful impact on improving energy performance and reducing costs for buildings, the use of eco-labels for energy codes. The Investment Facility, will support project owners and financial intermediaries in getting knowledge about the benefits of bioclimatic design (Activity 1.1.1). It will provide them with comprehensive information to ensure that they are able to implement green building practices (Activity 2.1.2). It is expected that the popularity of specifically designed products from the banking sector and related to green investments will result in a longer-term paradigm shift within both the consumer and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## Regulatory and institutional barriers: Lack of clear policies for building decarbonisation and insufficient building codes

Regulatory and institutional barriers that are frequently seen include:

- National climate change strategies and policies for the decarbonisation of sector lack clarity (e.g., lack of clear sectoral targets and specific actions).
- Building codes lack specific requirements for energy efficiency in buildings and enforcement is often weak, mandatory for certain types of buildings only, or still under discussion.
- Sector stakeholders are highly fragmented.

Barriers are further discussed in Section 3.1 of the Feasibility Study on a program level. Country-specific barrier analysis including market barriers are discussed in the country profiles located in the Annex of the Feasibility Study.

The Enabling Facility supports the building and construction sector stakeholders of the partner countries to establish national public-private alliances for energy efficient buildings, through the proposal and development of relevant policies (e.g., national cooling strategies for buildings, cooling action plans, etc.) and enabling measures (e.g. capacity building, technical assistance, etc.). Activities 2.1-2.3 aim at developing and implementing supportive policy frameworks, including supportive policies and energy efficient building codes (Activities 2.1 and 2.3). Because it takes its roots in the experience of the Investment Facility, the improvement of the policy framework will have higher chance to be implemented and enforced by the national authorities and stakeholders involved in the building sector – so ensure they account more fully for cooling demand / heat stress from both a mitigation and adaptation perspective.

The following risks are presented which might prevent the programme from being achieved (mitigation measures have been proposed in Section F of this Funding Proposal):

- The political priorities of partner countries are not aligned with PEEB Cool objectives in terms of energy efficiency.
- Insufficient availabilities and capacities among stakeholders at project and policy levels hinder or endanger Programme progress.
- Constraints on national commitments are too restrictive.

It has been assumed during the design of the program that market demand for bioclimatic housing will increase as prices decrease and stakeholders become more aware of the benefits. This is a critical assumption, because it assumes that there will be sufficient availability in the financial sector to offer suitable products to fund construction of cool buildings. This availability of finance will be critical to overcome higher costs of green buildings. It is also assumed that investors, developers, and other stakeholders are willing to invest in the development of cool buildings for green building sectors. Green buildings will complement and align with other priorities such as national climate change strategies, energy efficiency policies, etc., and will be aligned with the Investment Facility's approach which will build on synergies between green buildings and other sectoral and development priorities (e.g., housing, urban planning, etc.) in the partner countries.

Further. It is assumed that women and girls are given equal and proportionate access to sustainable cooling options. Where gender gaps exist, the programme will proactively address these through its Gender Action Plan (Annex B).

### Health (hospitals and health centres)
- **Vulnerability to heat stress:** Studies show that temperatures higher than 26°C have been associated with increased emergency room visits, especially during heatwaves.

- **Insulation and shading awareness through signage:** Add roof insulation and shading signs for visitors.

Education

Direct vulnerability due to reduced productivity in schools.

A 2018 study on thermal comfort and air quality in university buildings found that increased CO₂ and temperature exposure significantly lowered values recommended by ASHRAE. Researchers have calculated that for every 0.5°C increase in average temperature over the year, there is an 1% fall in learning**.

#### B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

**PEEB Cool objectives**
PEEB Cool aims to transform the construction sector by advancing less climate vulnerable and more energy-efficient building design, construction and operation. PEEB Cool prioritises sub-sectors with significant potential for climate change adaptation and GHG reduction such as large-scale new housing, public buildings, and commercial buildings. The Programme will support primarily new constructions, but it will also address refurbishment of existing building stock. As a cross-cutting programme, objectives have been set for both mitigation and adaptation.

The mitigation objective for the shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways is 1.563 million tCO2 eq reduced or avoided direct emissions as a result of the lifetime impact of buildings constructed by the end of the Programme's duration.

The adaptation objective for reduced vulnerabilities is 1.133 million direct beneficiaries (581,373 females).

**PEEB Cool approach**
PEEB Cool will bridge the theory to practice gap that exists in national and international programmes for transformation of the construction sector by supporting Subprojects in which local actors will

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 27

## In Component 1: Investment Facility, PEEB Coop supports local actors in implementing more resilient and energy efficient building projects with high-end beneficiary projects with more efficient construction methods.

### This is achieved through:
- (i) technical assistance at design phase to ensure that the project meets the requirements of the Programme
- (ii) concessional loans to address the financial barrier linked to higher investment costs and related risks, as well as capacity building for local actors in the implementation phase to monitor the proper implementation of bioclimatic design and energy efficiency measures and ensure the targeted direct impacts of the Programme are met.

### Component 2: Enabling Facility
- Combines technical assistance at design phase with financial instruments and regulations to perpetuate and scale up these good practices, while ensuring that capacities for implementing bioclimatic design and energy efficiency measures are built.
- This component also supports the implementation of new performance requirements in public procurement and sectoral investments.

Throughout its activities, PEEB Coop encompasses, where relevant, efficient cooling solutions, low carbon materials, and other innovative technologies to ensure a comprehensive approach (please refer to section 2.3 of the Feasibility Study for additional information on those three thematic areas).

#### 27.3 MEUR grant (22 MEUR from GCF and 4 MEUR from AFD Group)

##### Activity 1.1.1 Technical assistance support at identification and design phases

Technical Assistance at the design phase aims to review identified Subprojects and characterize their potential for adaptation, mitigation or both. The technical assistance will identify the measures that can be taken to achieve adaptation or mitigation targets. The technical assistance will describe the recommendations for each Subproject in terms of adaptation (e.g., renovation, new construction) relative to the baseline (baseline definition is explained in the section 4.3.1.2 of the Feasibility Study) as well as the economic feasibility and financial sustainability of these measures. The technical assistance offered to Subprojects at this stage consists of the preparation of technical, legal, and economic feasibility studies for each Subproject. These recommendations in the feasibility studies will aim to achieve the following targets for each Subproject type:

- Adaptation: either renovation or new construction. The Subproject addresses a specific climate-related risk (e.g., heat waves, flooding) by implementing measures that are not related to renovation. The target will be for building users (male/female) to have an improvement in their living conditions and/or health status as well as an increase in the comfort level of buildings (in buildings that are not mechanically cooled, an increase in 20% thermal comfort is a reduction in energy consumption compared to the baseline; or/and 15% water consumption savings compared to the baseline).
- Mitigation: targets in renovation Subproject 40% GHG emissions reduction or 40% energy consumption savings compared to the existing situation before renovation;
- Mitigation: targets in new construction Subprojects 20% GHG emissions reduction compared to the baseline; and/or 20% energy consumption savings compared to the baseline; and/or 20% water consumption savings compared to the baseline (a minimum of 2 out of 3 criteria).

Eligibility for this activity is based on the estimated potential for climate related impact and replicability of the Subproject (eligible criteria) and detail of the TA activities are described in the Feasibility Study.

PEEB Cool will help undertake the switch between flare gases and natural coolants for clean active cooling. As this aspect will be a major challenge in some countries during identification phase, the Activity 1.1.1 will also finance an assessment of the natural coolants implementation opportunities for each country. The feasibility study will include a detailed analysis of the potential climate study for each country. This study will be made at the start of the implementation of PEEB Cool.

## Activity 1.1.2 Technical assistance at construction and operation phase

All Subprojects that receive investment financing using GCF concessional loan co-financing (see Output 1.2) will receive technical assistance to support the TA. The TA will ensure that agreed measures are effectively implemented in the Subproject and the Subproject owner is effectively implemented at construction phase. The following types of technical assistance could be provided:

- Project management assistance for the implementation of recommended measures to improve the performance of the Subproject, including specific technical expertise;
- Support for verifications/ certifications.

## Activity 1.1.3 Capacity reinforcement to Subproject stakeholders

In addition, capacity reinforcement for project owners, financial intermediary and contractors will also be offered in order to ensure that they are able to add additional climate impacts for the Subprojects or future Subprojects; construction firms involved in the Subproject could benefit from training on the application of construction methods that are adapted to the climate context and local materials. This continues towards the upscaling of local actors and the development of a local offer of energy efficiency services.

Gender gaps in the green building sector include a lack of ownership of and control over buildings and associated resources, as well as weak representation of women in decision-making roles as product users, employees, entrepreneurs and leaders are weak. Actions and design interventions are required to improve gender outcomes and ensure gender mainstreaming in project activities. The Programme will support Subprojects that address the gender issues associated with accessing energy efficiency services. According to the Programme Guidance Note, capacity building on gender related topics will also be provided to identify staff from each project owner.

More comprehensive capacity development activities to favour local upscaling beyond the direct Project stakeholders for sectors in which financing Subprojects are identified, financed, or assisted in the construction phase will be provided through Output 2.3 in Component 2.

The cap per country is set at 20% of the total grant amount made available by GCF.

## Output 1.2: Financing arrangements for investments signed

- 941 MEUR and 328 MUSD loan (131 MEUR and 45 MUSD from GCF, and 830 MEUR and 281 MUSD from AFG Group)

## Activity 1.2.1 Investment funding

Implementation of measures for bioclimatic and energy efficient design of buildings induces higher investment costs compared to the baseline Subproject on average 10% based on PEEs experience. This is due to the fact that building users are often reluctant to accept incremental costs for green buildings. In order to address this barrier, it is proposed to support Subprojects in which the cost savings (operational and financial costs) of the green building off-setting the incremental up-front costs of passive and energy efficient measures. The Programme will support the following types of Subprojects:

## Technical criteria
a. Project type:
- Building construction or renovation
- Efficient cooling / heating infrastructure

Credit will be provided only if the project is undertaken in one of the eligible countries that allows for the financing of one or several subprojects of the two types defined above.

b. Targeted sub-sector, technologies or actors
- Housing – social (i.e., lower income) and middle income housing;
- Education (e.g., classrooms in rural areas; science complex, training centres);
- Health (health centres, hospitals);
- Public buildings and commercial buildings (public buildings, markets, shopping malls, offices);
- Industry;
- Transportation (train and bus stations);
- Tourism.

c. Technology type:
- Architectural solutions boosting natural ventilation and/or comfort inertia, thus passive cooling, and therefore the reduction of energy consumption linked to the cooling of buildings (e.g., compact building design, use of materials with good thermal properties, etc.)
- Sub-systems for active cooling systems using efficient cooling with low energy consumption (air handling units with energy recovery, smart ventilation units, cold coils & sharing, etc.).
- "Clean" active cooling using systems with an excellent coefficient of performance (compared to traditional systems in warm/cold countries), using clean coolants and, if possible, a low and renewable energy supply

## Minimum performance targets for investment criteria (for direct investment and investments in projects)
- Adaptation: The target will be for buildings (male/female) to have experienced an improvement in their indoor thermal conditions of at least 20% compared to the baseline or existing situation. An improvement in indoor thermal comfort is a reduction in 20% of the hours of discomfort resulting from improvements brought to the building.

A total of EUR 176 million of concessional loans is requested from CCF for the Investment Facility. These loans will be used by GCF to remove a major barrier of integrating bioclimatic and energy efficient measures in buildings. GCF's contribution will be used to off-set additional expenses from реконструкции зданий, which are not covered by the concessional loan. The cost of these expenses is additional to the Costowner. Additional elements in the use of CFC concessionality as described in Section 8.5 and below. These funds will be blended with EUR 1,111 million loan from AFD Concessions Facility (CCF) for the Investment Facility.

## Intermediated financing

Financial system stakeholders have a key role to play in financing investments that integrate energy efficiency and bioclimatic measures in all sectors (construction, industry, tertiary sector, infrastructure). The PEEB Cool eligible sectors can catalyse development of financial products for this market. The funds under the Investment Facility will be used by GCF to support the development of these financial products for financing projects which technical specifications are common to all end-beneficiaries and projects and are defined with the financial intermediary. Eligibility criteria for financial institutions and on-lending activities are described in Section 8.5 and below.

End-beneficiaries are the clients or prospects of the LFPIs, public or private-owned MSMEs, leasing companies, microfinance institutions, ...

For intermediated financing, the allocation of the funds to final beneficiaries are decided upon two possible ways: ex-post and ex-ante decisions.


---
**Page 36**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 34

- In ex-post, the End-beneficiaries projects pipeline is decided during formalisation process, as in direct financing. The LFP has to comply with the End-beneficiaries projects pipeline.
- In ex-post, the investment process fully belongs to the LFP. The LFP is audited during acquisition and implementation phases. The LFP must ensure that all criteria of Subproject eligibility criteria of End-beneficiaries projects is formalised. During implementation, the LFP reports a summary of the project (costs, benefits, impacts, project owners, etc.). On the basis of these reports, the LFP has to submit an allocation request for the line item “End-beneficiaries projects”. The EE Fund gives a “no objection” to the allocation of the credit line to the End-beneficiaries projects. The LFP must give a “no objection” which is required by the LFP or the EE, or to finance the End-beneficiaries projects.
- In case a project does not comply with the Subproject’s eligibility criteria, this proposed End-beneficiary project is financed by other funds (e.g., GCF). If the project complies with the Subproject’s eligibility criteria and the EE Fund is financing the LFPs which are financing the End-beneficiaries projects: The EE funds are financing the LFPs, which are financing the End-beneficiaries projects. The LFP is entirely managing the financing of End-beneficiaries projects.

Voluntary prepayment is going to be allowed. In case the funds are not invested within a certain amount of time (decided by the LFP), the prepaid amount will be deployed a second time on eligible projects.

Further details on intermediated financing with PEEB Cool are provided in the section 4.3.1.4 of the Feasibility Study.

## Determination of GCF funding support and concessionality for each Subproject (direct and intermediated financing)

To encourage and allow adoption of the bioclimatic and energy efficiency measures, PEEB Cool offers to mobilise GCF concessional loan as co-financing of AFD Group debt investment, both in direct and intermediated subprojects. The amount of GCF loan blended with AFD Group debt will respect two stringent criteria:

a. **Determination of GCF level of blended finance** based on the calculation of a grant-equivalent factor (GEF) which is defined as follows: GEF = 1 - (the difference in financial costs (interest and repayments)) between GCF financial terms (see proposed terms below) and AFD Group financial terms (or commercial terms, if no AFD Group debt is used). The GEF will be capped at a maximum of 50%. This mechanism aims to reduce the debt service related to the financing of the green building to un-lock its investment. It shall however never become lower than the initial debt service. The GEF will also take into account the incremental cost of energy efficiency improvements in green building standard. This will size the GCF blended contribution for each Subproject. The GCF loan amount is determined as a function of the GEF, so as to strictly off-set the additional debt service which is due to the incremental costs of bioclimatic and energy efficient measures without the support of the investment facility.

b. **GCF debt contribution** will be capped based on AFD Group climate finance principles for each Subproject. The GCF debt contribution will be determined by an ambitious methodology to evaluate the share of its financings bearing a climate co-benefit. This methodology is provided in section 4.3.1.5 of the Feasibility Study. Energy efficiency shall be achieved to be classified as climate finance. This methodology is provided in the section 4.3.1.5 of the Feasibility Study.


---
**Page 37**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FINANCING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

While the share of GCF debt financing shall never be higher than the share of climate co-benefit of the Subproject financings.

The conditions will be maintained by adjusting the share of co-financing from the GCF contribution to the Subproject in accordance with Article 28 applicable for each Subproject on whether the lender is an LDC and whether the lender is a public sector entity or a private institution. The remaining funds will be provided either by AFD for public-led Subproject or by PROPARCO for private-led Subprojects.

In all cases, the maturity will be up to 12 years and the grace period of the whole program up to 12 years[1]. The loan size will be capped based on climate accounting of the Subproject.

For intermediated financing, AFD ensures that the concessional financing will pass to the end users in terms of interest rate and grace period. The concessional financing for Subprojects supported by AFD is formalized in a framework agreement. Either the interest rate of end-users loans is capped itself according to an agreement made between AFD and LFs, or the margin of the LF is capped.

### Component 2: Enabling Facility

Component 2 consists of four output areas that are closely interlinked with the Investment Facility in component 1 and will run for five years. They contribute to setting a frame for a healthy and future-proof development of the Subproject area, including investments and encourage the replication of good practices implemented in component 1.

Key beneficiaries of the activities in component 2 include the following. A longer list of beneficiaries is detailed in chapter 4.3.2 of the Feasibility Study.
- Sectoral stakeholders such as public-private partnerships, alliances and their industry members or other public-private interest groups that will be supported in developing sectoral frameworks or other industry commitments
- Public institutions and agencies who receive support in designing and implementing regulations, standards, and programs.

### Component 2 consists of the following output areas:

#### Output 2.1: Sectorial investment frameworks unlock investment potential for green recovery

6.5 MEUR GCF grant, on average 0.59 MEUR per country.

This output supports further green buildings investments in the sectors targeted by the Investment Facility. This is enabled through sectoral roadmaps and actions plans. Thus it will support economic recovery from the COVID-19 crisis by addressing the following challenges: (i) high-perceived risk, lack of market information, and limited experiences with efficient and resilient building design and construction implementation; and (ii) reluctance of industry actors to adopt their services for the construction and operation of energy-efficient (EE), low carbon and resilient buildings specifically in times of economic crisis

[1] 12 years is the maximum amount of time before the 1st repayment of the latest loan that could be approved within PEBB Cool. The programmatic approach allows to approve subprojects until year 5, then to implement this subproject in phases over a period up to 7 years if and when each subproject generates incomes. Therefore, 12 years is the longest grace period that is possible in the program.

## Activity 2.1.1: Development of sectoral frameworks or initiatives

Within the framework of the Green Climate Fund (GCF), sectoral frameworks or initiatives with industry commitments will be supported. Construction-industry actors, represented by their federations are supposed to define commitments (e.g., green labels of construction sector products or services), action plans and roadmaps for the decarbonization of buildings and infrastructure, and the decarbonization of their businesses. These commitments, roadmaps etc., are the elements that form the sectoral framework or initiative.

The sectoral frameworks or initiatives will be supported by technical assistance and professional organizations encouraging the adoption of more demanding construction methods through labels and certifications (Effeicore, E+ C, Passivhaus, HGE, DGNB, LEED, BREEAM etc.). The sectoral frameworks or initiatives will also include action plans for the following:

- Investment roadmaps for the hospitals sector (Algeria), public buildings (Slovenia North Macedonia), residential buildings (Indonesia, Nigeria, Tunisia), educational buildings (Indonesia), district cooling sector (Tunisia), or the construction industry including local building materials and services (Morocco).

- Action plans for strengthening industry labels (Morocco)

Planned sub-activities include the following:

Decision on investment roadmap or industry initiative development: Based on the potential identified in the feasibility study stage of the financing projects, the sector stakeholders and socio-economic context will be mapped and analysed in order to identify the potential for additional investment, job creation, and decarbonization possibilities in this sector in the near future. The results will be used as a basis for defining an investment roadmap or industry action and industry commitments. General appetite and conditions for investments in the sector will also be identified at this stage of the feasibility study. This will allow for confirmation of collaboration with the industry sector partner federation, association, alliances etc.

Support on investment roadmap or industry initiative development: Based on the potential identified in the feasibility study stage of the financing projects, the sector stakeholders and socio-economic context will be mapped and analysed in order to identify the potential for additional investment, job creation, and decarbonization possibilities in this sector in the near future. The results will be used as a basis for defining an investment roadmap or industry action and industry commitments. General appetite and conditions for investments in the sector will also be identified at this stage of the feasibility study. This will allow for confirmation of collaboration with the industry sector partner federation, association, alliances etc.

The entire implementation of green buildings needs a supportive and incentivizing policy. Therefore, it is of utmost importance to support the development of policies that enable and stimulate green buildings in order to achieve sector transformation. The experiences from the financing of climate change mitigation projects under the Investment Facility have shown that such policies are essential for success.

The national contexts, providing valuable “on the ground” experiences for better public policies, national strategies, and enabling frameworks. The successful implementation of these policies will expand existing and create new markets for services or products and stimulate investment.

This output directly contributes to indicator 2.2.1 supports partner countries with advice on public policies in which the Investment Facility intervenes with funding Subprojects to anchor the Programme in an enabling framework and to deliver climate impact beyond the Programme duration (indicator 3.4).

Activity 2.2.1. Support for development of policies on energy efficiency in buildings

An important policy tool for the transformation of the building sector towards more sustainable, resilient and energy efficient construction is the adoption of building codes. However, in developing countries, such codes are often not fully applied (e.g., Nigeria: less than 50% of buildings comply with building codes; Costa Rica: less than 10%). International experience shows that successful building energy codes that are fully applied can reduce energy consumption by up to 40%. To ensure the adoption and full application of such codes, public policies will be developed (or updated), particularly in sectors where Subprojects are financed by the Investment Facility in order to deliver long-term climate impact. Within this context, the following activities will be supported:

- Development of building energy codes and their enforcement;
- Capacity building for government/municipal agents and developers; and
- Use of software tools to facilitate the application of building codes (linking existing or new building codes with national incentive programs to ensure their adoption).

Examples of likely policy concerns that are concerned by this activity are summarized as follows:

- Certification of buildings (Nigeria, Sri Lanka, Argentina);
- Improvement and enforcement of building code (Costa Rica, Djibouti, Indonesia, Mexico, Nigeria);
- Establishment of energy efficiency requirements for building sub-sectors such as health establishments (Indonesia), educational construction (Morocco), residential buildings (Indonesia) or social housing (Tunisia).

Plan sub-activities for this activity are as follows:

Agreement on support areas: National meetings among all project stakeholders will confirm the previous list of priority countries and subsectors and priorities intervention as well as assess concrete steps. This may be done through a checklist based on the WB RISE scores, and own experiences.

Provision of support: The PEEB Cool enabling facility team will set up a pool of consultants and mobilize expertise from the pool as requested and needed for the implementation of the identified

## Continuous coordination with investment projects and updating of relevant sectoral/training offer: Taking into account the diversity of participating countries and sectors in PEEB Cool, the existing training needs analysis will be continuously updated and clustered in order to provide a useful and practical training offer for all stakeholders involved in the project, including those from the Investment Facility.

### Development of 10 new training modules: Based on the continuous monitoring of the training needs, new training modules will be developed with internal and external expertise. The trainings will be developed with international best-practices and benchmarks, but will leave designated room for additions and adaptations to local contexts. In addition to the existing standards such as DGNB, HQE, LEED will be taken into consideration for use or adaptation into the national context. Examples from specific PEEB Cool investment subprojects that lead by example and provide a good practice for other projects in the same sector will also be considered opportunities within the sector.

### Series of some training modules to national authorities/institutions: Since all training modules will be developed in an "open-ended" mode, the training content will be, where possible handed-over to interested and relevant national authorities to continue offering trainings beyond PEEB Cool. This could also be used as starting points for more elaborate training programs or developed further into technical and vocational training activities that are ongoing in this respective country and sector.

## Activity 2.1.2 Implementation of training and awareness raising measures

The implementation of the developed trainings follows the logic defined by each country intervention and the most pressing market needs. To ensure further long-term usage and value to the partner countries, the content of training events shall be integrated, where possible, in national training programs and curricula, as well as in technical and vocational training activities that are ongoing in the respective country and sector.

### Sub-activities to be implemented include the following:

* **Setting up of a training schedule:** A training schedule will be agreed upon with the partner organizations. The training schedule should align with the investment subprojects, Investment roadmaps, and public policies to be supported.
* **Local adaptation:** While the overall modules will be developed from an international best practice standard, they can also be adapted locally in order to better fit the local context. This may involve national/regional/international consultants that will implement the trainings, with the support of the local PEEB Cool team locally.

## Implementation of trainings: The trainings will be implemented locally in a face-to-face setting where possible. Online formats may complement but not replace certain trainings if the circumstances are favourable. Due to past experiences on the effectiveness and learning outcomes of participants, it is recommended that only a limited number of participants attend each training session. This allows for very tailored learning but requires a careful selection of participants. This selection will be done in close cooperation with the local PEEB Cool team locally.

### Evaluation sheets: In that point of view, the PEEB Cool team locally. Evaluation sheets, that is in particular look at relevance of the training for market development of energy efficient and resilient buildings, will be administered and evaluated.

As to the stakeholders involved in the training sessions, they can include 1) project owners (including ministries, other public entities and private developers), architects/designers and construction companies and 2) financial intermediaries.

## Output 2.4: Programme experience supports the global building sector transformation

1 MEUR grant, 0.5 MEUR from GCF and 0.5 MEUR from BMU/BWK

This output will build upon the Programme's experience and make information available regionally and internationally through a knowledge product development process, dissemination of lessons learned and dissemination at the level of the entire Program (components 1 and 2). Another envisaged result is to support the identification and financing of energy efficient and resilient buildings investments, as well as innovative financial models and de-risking™ and upscaling tools. This output contributes to the indicator 2.4.

### Activity 2.4.1 Development of knowledge products

PEEB Cool PMU, in collaboration with PEEB and Ending Facilities will generate a wealth of experiences that will be channelled in different knowledge products and thus made accessible to a wider audience. It is envisaged to put a focus on successes that successful de-risk investment in energy efficient buildings can achieve through the use of innovative financial instruments available to national governments and development finance institutions at the example of PEEB Cool PMU experiences.

Planned sub-activities include the following:

* Continuous monitoring of project results and experiences worth transforming into knowledge products: regular meetings at HQ or PMU level will serve to consolidate total PEEB Cool experience and feed it into a knowledge pipeline.
* Development of knowledge products: The knowledge products will be developed based on the different program activities. These build the foundation for making project experiences available within and beyond PEEB Cool.

### Activity 2.4.2 Dissemination of experience

PEEB Cool PMU will feed its implementation experience into the joint effort to decarbonise the buildings and construction sector at the national and international level by making its knowledge products and experiences accessible to Subproject implementers and investors, and a wider audience via regional and international platforms (e.g. International Development Finance Club, GlobalABC, UNFCCC events, COPs).

Planned sub-activities include the following:

* Monitoring of dissemination occasions: This will be a continuous activity in order to identify the most interesting opportunities to disseminate what PEEB Cool experiences and knowledge products could be presented.
* Preparation and presentation of inputs in diverse formats: The PEEB Cool PMU or local staff will regularly participate in relevant forums and disseminate relevant experiences throughout the entire duration of PEEB Cool.

PEEB Cool will be implemented jointly by the following three institutions:

- AFD, as accredited entity, executing entity and co-financer for the Investment Facility (financial incentives and technical assistance) targeting public sector Subproject owners (Component 1).
- PROPARCO, as executing entity and co-financer for the Investment Facility (financial incentives and technical assistance) targeting private sector (Component 1).
- GIZ, as executing entity for activities within the Enabling Facility (Component 2).

## As accredited entity, AFD will host the Programme Management Unit (PMU) and manage the contractual relation with the GCF.

The three entities will normally coordinate through the Steering Committee, which will be chaired by the GCF representative in Paris. In addition, AFD will have a PEEB Cool investment facility implementation within the public sector. AFD project teams will be responsible for the identification, appraisal and implementation of eligible investments.

### Subprojects' appraisal process will follow AFD's procedures.

#### PROPARCO will be in charge of PEEB Cool implementation within the private sector.
As per AFD projects, PROPARCO project teams will be responsible for the identification, appraisal, implementation and monitoring of eligible investments. The appraisal process will follow AFD's procedure.

### In particular, due diligence from AFD Group will carry out due diligences on appraisals on each Subproject in accordance with AFD Group standards and procedures.
Due diligences include KYC and anti-money laundering policies, and E&S standards.

#### GIZ will be responsible for the implementation of activities within the Enabling Facility (component 21 - GIZ) by using a combination of own staff based in headquarters and respective partner countries as well as independent consultants. All will report to the PEEB Cool Programme Management Unit.

### Governance arrangement and programme management
A Programme Management Unit will coordinate the overall implementation of the Programme, while a Steering Committee will ensure that PEEB Cool remains consistent across Subprojects.
The Programme Management Unit will be supported by a Programme Team which will bring technical expertise.

- **PEEB Cool** will be monitored by a Programme Management Unit (PMU) established at AFD's headquarters in Paris. The Programme Management Unit will consist of three full time programme managers.
- The PMU will be responsible for structuring, implementing and monitoring the Programme and will:
  - Technical and administrative supervision of the Programme;
  - Coordination of the Programme’s activities;
  - Supervision/implementation of eligible Projects via expertise and technical assistance mobilisation;
  - Communication activities.

### Programme Team
The PMU will be supported by a team of AFD Group experts consisting of an environmental and social specialist, a climate specialist, an energy efficiency specialist, a private sector specialist, a legal expert, a procurement expert, and a finance specialist. They will not be full-time staff but will be available on a full-time basis for the duration of the project and will act as part of a pool of experts, providing advice in their field of expertise when needed.

### AFD and PROPARCO staff based in Paris or in local offices will follow up preparation and implementation of each eligible Subproject identified under the Investment Facility. GIZ will mainly coordinate with AFD and PROPARCO on the implementation of the Enabling Facility locally in every country of intervention.
Expertise France (EF), AFD's affiliate for technical cooperation, may also intervene in the implementation of the Enabling Facility.

# Contractual arrangements

* The GCF and AFD will sign a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) and at GCF funds, whether reimbursable or non-reimbursable, will be transferred from the GCF to AFD according to the provisions set in the FAA. The FAA will be a grant and sub-participation.
* AFD will sign a solidarity agreement with GCF and will channel GCF funds to GCF for the purpose of implementing Subprojects.
* AFD will sign a subsidiary agreement with PROPARCO, which will take the form of a mandate agreement that will define the fiduciary and implementation arrangements between AFD and PROPARCO.

## Financing agreement between AFD or PROPARCO and the beneficiaries

For direct financing
Eligible Subprojects within AFDF’s public sector (and PROPARCO’s scope private sector) will benefit from financial support extended by AFD and PROPARCO through the Investment Facility (Component 1) on a subject-by-subject basis. For this purpose, PROPARCO and AFD will sign financing agreements with the respective implementing bodies (developers, ministries, etc.). Financial arrangements will be defined in accordance with the terms of reference for each Subproject for the purpose of implementing the Subprojects, and will be developed and negotiated according to AFD’s credit and procurement procedures.

For intermediated finance
Loan agreements will be signed between AFD and public financial intermediaries, and between Proparco and private financial intermediaries. The loan agreement will specify the eligibility criteria, including the maximum amount of financing per beneficiary.

The eligibility criteria will be defined at conception stage by the technical assistant (Output 1.1) but will at least abide by the eligibility criteria of direct financing Subprojects. Based on the targeted beneficiaries and local context, the technical assistant will prepare technical specification for eligible investments (construction for developers or acquisition for households) and structure a financial offer in line with the terms of reference for each Subproject.

For example, in Peru, AFD and Fonds Movilvenda have launched a new product called “a green mortgage” which allows households to benefit from a subsidy if their home is certified, making the price of green housing equal to the price of conventional housing for certified homes.

AFD will work in priority with national accredited entities, wherever and whenever it is suitable. It includes, but not limited to, the following national accredited entities identified in PEEB Cool countries:
