# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## AFD
**Agence Française de Développement (AFD) / French Development Agency is a financial institution and the main implementing agency for France's official development assistance to developing countries. The AFD budget amounts to EUR 12 billion, of which EUR 8 billion are grants and commitments, 6.1 billion of which are designed for positive impact on the climate. Regarding energy efficiency, AFD has been involved in two large-scale programmes (SUNREEF and PEEB) that have shown encouraging results worldwide.**

Energy efficiency constitutes one of the three focuses of AFD's energy transition strategy 2015-2020. In 2019, AFD invested EUR 34 million in energy efficiency including EUR 346 million of direct financed subprojects, EUR 600 million public policy lending with energy efficiency component and EUR 444 million of intermediated financing.

AFD has local offices in 12 of the 13 PEEB Cool countries included in this proposal and has construction and retrofitting experience (education, health, energy sectors) in most of them.


## PROPARCO
**PROPARCO (Société de Promotion et du Particulier à la Coopération Économique) is an affiliate of AFD focused on private sector development. It has been promoting sustainable economic, social and environmental-development practices for the past 40 years. As a development finance institution, PROPARCO finances projects in the energy sector (electricity generation, transmission and distribution), to boost the contribution of the private sector to achieving the sustainable development goals.**

In 2018, PROPARCO approved EUR 2.5 billion, increasing its outstanding portfolio at EUR 4.6 billion with over 500 clients in 119 countries. With a growing interest in embedded energy efficiency in projects, PROPARCO has been supporting the development of new technologies and innovative solutions for constructions of buildings within health, education, tourism, infrastructure, and industrial sectors; Greenfield projects (e.g., wind farms) as well as expansions of production capacities or greenfield projects with actors in the construction ecosystem.

PROPARCO is financing operations in all PEEB Cool countries and has physical presence in 5 of them (Morocco, Nigeria, Tunisia, Mexico, and Indonesia). The remaining countries are coordinated from the closest regional office ensuring on-the-ground presence.


## GIZ
As a service provider in the field of international cooperation for sustainable development and international education work. GIZ has over 50 years of experience in a wide variety of areas, including economic development, social development, environmental protection, and climate security. GIZ works with businesses, civil society actors and research institutions, fostering successful partnerships to achieve sustainable development goals.

In 2017, GIZ generated a business volume of around 3 billion euros and employed 20,726 persons, almost 70 per cent of whom are national personnel in 120 countries.

GIZ is present with national and international staff in the 13 PEEB Cool countries included in this proposal (Albania, Djibouti, Egypt, Indonesia, Iraq, Jordan, Morocco, Nigeria, Pakistan, Tunisia, Vietnam, Nigeria, Mexico, Indonesia). Ongoing GIZ bilateral projects in the energy sector advise partners in the area of energy efficiency and renewable energies. GIZ also supports national institutions to develop strategies or global projects in or related to the energy sector, implemented by GIZ and its partners (Argentina, Albania, Djibouti, North Macedonia, Sri Lanka, Mali).

B.8 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

GCF’s support is critical to address barriers to energy efficiency in buildings and move towards a paradigm shift: access to affordable finance.

GCF funding is essential to address the key barrier to energy efficiency in buildings and move towards a paradigm shift: access to affordable finance.

Despite their enormous mitigation and adaptation potential, measures to reduce overheating and increase cooling capacity are not being widely adopted. A significant proportion of climate investment particularly in developing countries. The PEEB Cool feasibility Study highlighted the fact that the market for cool buildings is underdeveloped because of a lack of financing systems. PEEB Cool countries is not mature enough to expand without financial incentives. These activities will be perceived by Suppliers owners as complex and unattractive and the environmental advantage and water savings are often seen as secondary benefits. The cost of energy efficiency investments in buildings is often subject to split incentives when the entities making the investments in buildings are different from those using the building (and experiencing the benefits of reduced energy bills and increased comfort).

GCF concessional loans address this financial barrier by enabling PEEB Cool to lower the total cost of the improved building cooling service at a cost close to that of a standard building. Such buildings which will be built or retrofitted with cool building technologies. Furthermore, GCF resources will incentivise the public sector in particular to invest in more sustainable and environment friendly buildings. The PEEB Cool feasibility Study shows that in order to reach a 50% reduction in energy consumption compared to baseline performance, 315 MEUR in grants would be needed to cover the incremental costs of construction and refurbishment. PEEB Cool ensures minimum concessionality by financing these investments at concessional rates. The GCF will provide concessional loans with interest rates from the high concessionally of GCF loans. Further description of the way the financial structure, including the terms and conditions of the loan, is described in the feasibility Study and in the explanatory note annexed to the Economic and Financial Analysis.

Synergies between activities financed by grants and those financed by debt

Investments alone are not sufficient to transform the building sector at a national level. The GCF will support the development of a sectoral investment framework combined with the development of a sectoral investment framework, adapted public policies, technical assistance and capacity building. The GCF is the only source of funding that can provide both debt and the amount of financing required for the transformation of the building sector at national level. PEEB Cool will provide countries with both financial and technical tools to invest in a low-carbon building sector consistent with their NDCs.

A critical mass of investments is needed for market transformation (paradigm shift) to green buildings

In the past, energy-efficiency investments have been made on a case-by-case basis, treating each project as isolation. The ongoing PEEB programme approached the issue differently by pooling knowledge and experience into a common platform.

In addition, by allowing the application of this approach on key transformative Subprojects, the GCF funded PEEB Cool. This will allow for the scaling up of energy efficiency investments in buildings initiated by PEEB. PEEB Cool will thus be able to achieve a critical mass of bioclimatic investments which, supported by the creation of an enabling environment in the targeted countries, will lead to the desired paradigm shift. Without the scale and concessionality of finance that the GCF provides,

### Justification of concessionality of GCF concessional loans

GCF concessional loans will be blended with AFD and Propco funding to address the financial barrier (comprised of investment cost and risk premium) of EE investments. The co-financing ratio is determined based on a grant-equivalent concessionality calculation, which takes into account the evaluation of the development of the Subproject pipeline in the next four years. The diagram below displays past and current PEEB projects committed between 2018 and 2020. The climate finance amount for each project is calculated as the sum of the AFD contribution (50%) and the GCF contribution to AFD methodology (summarized in the section 4.3.1.5 of the Feasibility Study). This methodology, which is based on a grant-equivalent concessionality calculation, allows the identification of projects with certain impacts thresholds. The forecast includes projects that are under appraisal and identification at AFD and that AFD has committed in 2021. The diagram shows the steady increase in finance committed, which is the basis for which the GCF contribution level will be determined.

| 300 | 500 | 700 | 900 |
| 500 | 700 | 900 | 1,000 |

To encourage and allow adoption of the bioclimatic and energy efficiency measures, PEEB Cool offers to mobilize GCF concessional loan as co-financing of AFD Group debt investment. Minimum concessionality of the GCF contribution is ensured through the following considerations (further detail in the annexes):

a. Determination of GCF level of blended finance based on the calculation of a grant-equivalent concessionality: The grant equivalent of the GCF contribution is defined as


---
**Page 51**
---

The difference in financial costs (interest+repayments) between GCF financial terms (see proposed terms below) and AFD Group financial terms (or commercial terms available locally). The grant-equivalent concessionally provided to each Subproject will be capped at 50% of the total project cost, with a view to avoiding any unjustified use of this financial incentives. The proposed mechanism aims to reduce the overall costs of green building projects by leveraging concessional investment. It shall however never become lower than the initial debt service the owner would have had to pay under commercial terms (i.e., without the grant building standard). This will size the GCF blended contribution for each Subproject. In other words, the GCF debt contribution will be calculated as so as to strictly off-set the additional cost of financing the project with concessional terms compared to energy efficient measures without the support of the investment facility.

b. GCF Debt Contribution: The GCF will provide concessional finance principles for energy efficiency in buildings: AFD Group has developed a detailed, stringent and ambitious methodology to evaluate the share of its financings bearing a climate co-benefit. This means that only 50% of the total project cost financed by AFD Group of energy efficiency shall be achieved to be classified as climatic finance. This methodology is described in detail in the GCF Technical Document (GCF TD) and Annex 3c. It is proposed that the share of GCF debt contribution shall never be higher than the share of climate co-benefit of the Subproject financings.

The conditions for concessional financing are as follows: The GCF will provide co-financing from the GCF contribution, offering 5 applicable fixed interest rates, based on whether the country is an LDC and whether the lender is a sovereign, a public entity or a private-owned entity (the proposed rates are set out in Annex 3c). These concessional terms will be provided either by AFD Group for publicly-funded Subprojects or by Proparco for privately-led Subprojects.

The diagram below illustrates how GCF concessional loans will be structured as part of bioclimatic and energy efficient measures. This will enable the Programme to offer financing conditions under which green building Subprojects will be viable while avoiding crowding out other investors.

# Investment Facility approach to incremental cost of bio-climatic and energy efficient measures integration

## B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1/2 page)

The Subprojects are designed in close consultation with the sector and Subproject stakeholders (developers, planners and construction companies), to ensure their ownership of the Subproject both during the implementation and after its completion. The Enabling Facility is designed to future-proof the building sector development beyond other investment opportunities.

This programme will be using private sector developers for the buildings work. Since the GCF funding will enable more buildings to be available at more affordable prices, this will help stimulate the market by increasing demand and encouraging more private companies into the sector. Competition between developers will drive down transaction costs (e.g., fees, legal costs, systems driving prices down further and establishing a new norm in the green buildings market once the programme has ended). As transaction costs fall, there will be lower entry costs for Subproject developers, which will encourage more to enter the market (with greater access to supply, knowledge and expertise). The programme would therefore expect to see an acceleration of the green buildings market as the programme closes.

Mobilizing private sector actors into developing companies, with the support of policy and institutional changes, will create a paradigm shift that is lacking in the sector. The establishment of sectoral frameworks and initiatives, such as green labels of products and services, will support long-term sustainability. The Subprojects will also be able to benefit from GCF emissions from the cooling sector will continue to decline after the Subproject has closed. Policy and institutional changes will ensure emissions reductions are also expanded going forward.

Financial Intermediaries and banks will gain expertise and knowledge during Component 1 (investment facility), enhancing opportunities for long-term, sustainable investments. The realistic implementation of Subprojects will be a key driver in demonstrating that GCF can deliver demand. This will stimulate banks to increase the availability of financing options. The market will be kick-started and expanded during the Subproject’s completion.

Pre-screening of loan applications and evaluation of progress and monitoring of repayments will ensure the Subprojects meet the minimum post-completion of the programme. Qualifying Subprojects need to meet the eligibility criteria to ensure the long-term viability of the Subproject, including financial sustainability, environmental performance and social considerations. AIFD/PROPARCO will report on the disbursement of the loans to the GCF, and the input required from GCF will be minimal. The exit risk is minimal (Maturity of loans is usually 20 years with a 5-year grace period and the implementation period is usually 9 years).

# C3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)

## C3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building?

Yes ☑ No ☐

## C3.2 Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer?

Yes ☑ No ☐

The Programme includes extensive stakeholder engagement, notably through knowledge transfer and capacity building activities that are delivered directly in capacity building include Subproject owners and other Subproject stakeholders, building regulations, policy makers and local government officials responsible for building regulations, policy makers and local government officials responsible for building regulations, and representatives of companies in the buildings and construction value chain and verification offices.

Capacity building activities will be conducted at Subproject level and project level:

At Subproject level, project technical assistance will be hired for the whole Programme and will be mobilised through purchase orders to perform tailor-made activities for Subproject owners. Among other things, this will include capacity building in terms of knowledge transfer and use of Subproject owner capacities and, if needed, technical assistance activities to improve technical capacities of Subproject owners and stakeholders such as local companies directly involved in the Subproject's implementation.

At policy level, capacity building will be offered to representatives from both the public and the private sector, to complement the activities in the Investment Facility and in particular to ensure sufficient capacity for the implementation of the enabling framework. The proposed investment frameworks will be combined with capacity building activities for stakeholders such as architects, engineers. Previous training needs analyses in PEEB countries have already led to the identification of a short list of key technical capacities that need to be developed or expanded within PEEB CoE. Local authorities are targeted notably with trainings related to their authority and responsibilities (e.g., building regulations, urban planning, land use, and control of building permits). Together with partner institutions representation of all genders will be ensured in the training sessions.

As an example, Subproject activities funded from PEEB CoE financing often require compliance with national and/or international certifications or building standards. In this case, this output may support the national capacity building process for local partners (e.g., industry standardisation bodies) to ensure that their products comply with IEGE. LEED will be given consideration for use or adoption into the national context. Specific investment Subprojects will seek to demonstrate how they apply IEGE standards and thus create practical learning opportunities for the sector.

The Enabling Facility (Component 2) includes the following capacity building activities for which GCF funding is requested:

- Development and delivery of training and awareness raising measures as described above – on average less than 0.47 MEUR per country

Stakeholder engagement more broadly will also target interested civil society organizations, the construction ecosystem and other relevant actors to participate in activities such as the national stakeholder meetings that will be encouraged to benefit from the knowledge products developed under this project. The information will be made available regionally and internationally through a variety of knowledge products.

PEEB Cool is expected to provide technology transfer through the following actions (included within output 1.1):

- Sharing knowledge on implementing bioclimatic design adapted to the climate context of the Sub-Saharan region
- Sharing knowledge on implementing low carbon materials taking into account local material available in the Sub-Saharan region
- Sharing know-how on implement efficient cooling technologies in hot climates.
- Sharing know-how on simulation tools, for instance thermal dynamic simulations.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This appendix relates to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

As a cross-cutting programme, PEEB Cool will contribute to both mitigation and adaptation. The Programme is expected to:

- **Outcome 1:** Stimulate investments in the green building market
- **Outcome 2:** Improved resilience of the most vulnerable populations as buildings are better adapted to climate change
- **Outcome 3:** Reduced GHG emissions from buildings in the residential, education, health, and commercial sectors
- **Outcome 4:** Improved regulatory frameworks include national energy efficiency regulations, construction standards and certification commitments by industry actors, as well as training and increased awareness of human resources

Below we describe the expected mitigation and adaptation impacts of the Programme.

Given the fact that PEEB Cool is a multi-country programme, its impact in terms of both mitigation and adaptation was analysed following a portfolio approach. The analysis is based on an existing portfolio of large-scale building construction/renovation projects currently financed or to be financed by the AFD Group (Côte d’Ivoire, Senegal, Burkina Faso, Mali, Morocco, Tunisia). These include residential buildings, offices, commercial centers, hospitals, schools, small office buildings, hotels and retail. Countries targeted for the analysis were selected based on their geographical location: equatorial, tropical, humid subtropical, arid, Mediterranean, continental, and mountain climate.

### Mitigation impact - GHG emissions reduction at the scale of the whole Programme:

- **Direct impacts**
  - Direct mitigation impacts are due to reduced GHG emissions from buildings whose construction or renovation is financed by the PEEB Cool Programme.
  
**Baseline:** The baseline for a specific Subproject describes the expected thermal performance and energy consumption in terms of heating, cooling, ventilation, lighting, and appliances. This analysis is described in Section 4.3.12 of the Feasibility Study. Building simulation in the EEDeG App® or other, more detailed building thermal dynamic simulation software is then employed to model both the baseline case and the scenario case.

**Methodology for estimation of mitigation benefits:**

- **Baseline scenario:** The analysis first estimated the emissions per m² by building type in each of the climate zones covered by projects financed by PEEB Cool based on the baseline / standard construction and technology uses. This baseline is informed by an analysis of the common practices in construction and operation of buildings in these countries, including the typical materials used for demand, and the embodied energy in the construction materials. In some countries, there are no common practices that take efficiency considerations into account for cooling and motors use.
- **With Programme scenario:** The analysis then estimated the emissions per m² for the same buildings using more energy efficient design practices, equipment, and lower-emissions construction.

[1] https://eedebuildings.com/

**Page 58**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 56

## Metrics and Scenarios

Two baseline cases were examined – where the ongoing energy consumption was approximately 20% less and where the ongoing energy consumption was 40% less. The “with Programme” scenario involved averaging these two cases – assuming that across the portfolio there will be a mix of the two.

Both the Baseline and With Programme energy consumption (in kWh/m²) and GHG emissions (in CO₂eq/m²) were scaled up according to an estimate of 2% for each type of building to have investment in low-carbon materials. To reflect the ongoing energy consumption, it is assumed that the energy and GHG values would be calculated for a period of 15 years – which is more than the typical lifespan of a new building but reflective of the GCF’s recommendations.

The avoided emissions are then calculated as the difference between the Baseline and With Programme scenarios.

The construction phase accounts for anywhere from 42% to 63% of emissions reductions based on the fast-track analysis carried out for our programme preparation. This shows that low carbon materials can play a significant part and represent short-term GHG emission reductions.

A summary of the baseline emissions and emissions reductions from the Subproject list studied is provided below:

## Indirect Impacts

PEEB Cool approach is based on a scale up of good practices implemented in the Investment Facility. Thanks to the Enabling Facility, those new performance requirements can be generalized at national level.

It is assumed that PEEB Cool will support the improvement of the building code of each country that have the enabling facility incorporated into their law. The adoption of these countries makes it easier its replicate a subproject meeting the PEEB Cool specifications.


## Adaptation impact

As described in Section B.1, temperature increases as a result of climate change in the targeted countries, which will affect thermal comfort in buildings. Current building infrastructure is sensitive to such climate-induced changes, meaning indoor temperatures often reach levels above the comfort threshold defined using the ASHRAE 55-2017 Thermal Environmental Conditions for Human Occupancy standard.

To minimize vulnerability, adaptation measures such as bioclimatic design to improve ventilation and cooling, use of energy efficient appliances, and limited energy consumption. Both actions can reduce health risks, heat exposure and economic loss.


Concerning the overall pathway, improved thermal comfort will decrease climate vulnerability in the morbidity and mortality rates due to increased heat, fewer days for hospitalization, increased productivity in the workplace, and reduced air conditioning costs.

Climate vulnerability in the residential sector is associated with increased indoor temperatures leading to respiratory, core temperature, blood glucose, mental health, and physical functioning clinical health effects. 

Improvement of the thermal comfort will lower such impacts that have also been associated with an increased proportion of respiratory distress calls.


Concerning adaptation benefits in the health sector, the improvement of thermal comfort helps maintain patient comfort during hospital stays, which is a key factor for recovery. It can also reduce factors that influence the transmission and survivability of potential airborne pathogens such as bacteria, viruses, and fungi, which may pose a threat to patients.

In a case study in Indonesia [28] in the collective bedroom of a healthcare centre, in the baseline building 82% of occupancy hours are outside the comfort zone with a ceiling fan in the 20th decade. In a green building, a ceiling fan is sufficient to maintain comfort throughout the year. Should air conditioning be installed?

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 58

## INSTALLATION OF COOLING POWER REQUIRED WOULD DROP FROM 71 kW/m² TO 47 kW/m² IN A 2050 CLIMAX, IN THE BASELINE BUILDING THE MAXIMUM INDOOR OPERATIVE TEMPERATURE REACHES ALMOST 36°C AND ALMOST ALL OCCUPANCY HOURS ARE UNCOMFORTABLE, WHILE IN A GREEN BUILDING THE MAXIMUM TEMPERATURE IS REDUCED TO 32°C AND OCCUPANCY HOURS ARE COMFORTABLE. THE COST OF THE BASELINE BUILDING IS €572 EUR/m² AND THE BASELINE HEALTH CENTER IS €572 EUR/m² AND THE CONSTRUCTION COST OF THE IMPROVED PROJECT IS €613 EUR/m², BRINGING THE INCREMENTAL COST OF THE ENERGY EFFICIENCY MEASURES TO 7%.

## NUMBER OF DIRECT AND INDIRECT BENEFICIARIES

Direct beneficiaries are defined as the users of buildings whose construction or renovation is financed by the Programme. In addition to the Programmes, in buildings without mechanical cooling PEEB Cool support will lead to an increase of at least 20% in thermal comfort of the building, and often this number will be much higher.

To assess the number of beneficiaries for PEEB Cool, the Subproject portfolio that was analyzed was broken down by building type and then by direct beneficiaries for each building. The expectation is that any new build building will have a lifespan of 25 years – but with a direct impact from the Programme on the first five years.

In the residential sector the beneficiaries were calculated based on the number of households to be built/reinforced in each county multiplied by the members of household in each country taking into consideration the average occupancy density per dwelling. The number of direct beneficiaries is the number of dwellings assuming 60 m² per dwelling (i.e. 2/60 x 160) x number of people per household in specific countries.

Educational sector beneficiaries were assessed based on an average occupancy density of 5m²/person. It is assumed that a child will remain in the same school for 5 years so for a period of 15 years the number of direct beneficiaries is (the number of m2 / 5 m2 per person) x 3.

Direct beneficiaries of the health sector were assessed based on the number of people served by healthcare services. The number of people served in each region covered and therefore unique, even though they could use the healthcare services several times within the 15 year programme period is (the number of m2 / 60 m2 per person) x 3 x (number of people per household in a specific country / number of hospital = population served per hospital (i.e. direct beneficiaries).

Similarly to the case of education, for commercial use (retail buildings), beneficiaries have been based on the number of people served by the retail sector. The average age is 28 years old. It was assumed that during the 15 year period 1 business would be used as a space twice so the number of direct beneficiaries is (the number of m2 / 28 m2 per person).

For offices beneficiaries have been based on the default occupancy density the ESEE app of 30 m²/person. It was assumed that during the 15 year period 2 different people would use the same space (i.e. approximately 12 year average usage). The number of direct beneficiaries is (the number of m2 / 28 m2 per person) x 2.

Bueno, A.M., de Paula Xavier, A.A., Brodie, E.E. Evaluating the Connection between Thermal Comfort and Productivity in Buildings: A Systematic Literature Review. Buildings 2021, 11, 244

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 59

While most beneficiaries have been accounted for within a timeframe of 15 years (from year 1 to year 15), the indirect beneficiaries have been accounted for within the following 10-year timeframe (from year 16 to year 25). For example, taking the education beneficiaries example in Costa Rica:

- Direct: (number of m2 / m2 per person) x3 i.e. over years from 1 to 15: 78,800/5 = 39,438 people
- Indirect: (number of m2 / m2 per person) x2 i.e. over years from 16 to year 25: 78,800/5 = 39,438 people

The same approach is adopted for residential, office and retail beneficiaries. For health, indirect beneficiaries are assumed as zero.

The number of indirect beneficiaries has been computed only on the education sector – as the impacts are only felt in the first year period (i.e., within the first 15 years). As the climate change impacts could occur (even) after the respective sector, this was not estimated so as to use a conservative approach. The total number of direct beneficiaries of the PEEB Cool Programme has been estimated at 1,133 million people. The total number of indirect beneficiaries of the Programme is 33,620 people.

Further details on the methodologies used to calculate both the mitigation and the adaptation impact at the Programme level are provided in Annex A (Chapter 4) and in the list of Individual Subprojects are provided in the Feasibility Study (Chapter 5), in Annex B2 and in Annex C2.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 900 words, approximately 1 page)

The Programme will contribute to a paradigm shift across a number of different countries and even globally – wherein increasing attention will be paid to cooling and heating needs when designing and financing buildings. The PEEB Cool programme is designed in such a way that it can be scaled up over time when demand is increasing for cooling as temperatures rise. PEEB Cool will reduce emissions from air conditioning, which are currently responsible for 10% of global energy consumption, while also improving the thermal comfort of its beneficiaries and consequently their climate resilience.

Specifically, the programme will target clean cooling in developing countries and emerging economies in hot climates, using sustainable building construction materials that make the most out of locally available resources. The PEEB Cool Programme is designed to be replicable across a number of different countries; the potential of PEEB Cool can leveraged across other parts of the developing world.

Learning from the experience with the PEEB Cool programme in Costa Rica, it has been found that using cooler temperatures than conventional buildings, cool buildings can increase the resilience to temperature extremes. In addition, by reducing the need for air conditioning and heating, cool building design also reduces cooling demand (on average 20 to 30% reduction in hot climates). Aside from bioclimatic design, other innovative strategies of the PEEB Cool programme include integrating energy efficient appliances such as LED lighting, solar water heaters, and heat pumps that are renewable energy to power cooling systems, and low carbon district cooling networks. Energy efficiency requirements for buildings can be achieved by increasing the use of passive building up to 30 to 40%. This will be done through a few main mechanisms as detailed below:

Scaling up and replication of financing - As shown in the theory of change (section C.2), by demonstrating that cool buildings are not only more energy efficient but also provide significant energy-efficient air conditioning and appliances, and on-site renewable energy production, the Investment Facility (Component 1) will remove barriers related to lack of experience with and investment in cool building design. The Investment Facility will also support the development of a replicable and scalable financing model for green buildings. In Component 1, credit lines especially through local banks combined with technical assistance will generate a pipeline of bankable


---
**Page 62**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

## Subprojects demonstrating the potential of this market segment to local financial institutions.

Component 2 supports sustainable financing through awareness raising and capacity building of the financial sector on the subject, together with the creation of partnerships that can support the development of a market for green buildings in the region. The PEEB Cool Subprojects will be absorbed by the market without the use of subsidies once the initial "triggering" effect has been achieved. As awareness increases and more people become aware of the benefits professionals, and consumers recognize that investment in green buildings – with a special focus on cooling demand reduction – is an attractive option for their personal or professional lives, increasing comfort and value of real estate. This paradigm shift can be expected to occur in the products offered by financial institutions and in the considerations for future investment. There is a large potential market for PEEB Cool Subprojects as there are many people who have an urgent need for more public facilities such as schools and clinics. Population growth and rapid urbanization mean that demand will continue to increase, especially in countries where housing and educational buildings are only going to intensify in the coming decades. At the same time, a transformation is needed; since in many of the Sub-Saharan African countries even the short-term needs for new public facilities are not being met by existing building stock.

## Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

With a 50% increase in global floor area expected by 2030 (compared to 2016), and with the increasing number of developing countries (such as Indonesia and Nigeria), the change in construction practices encouraged by the Programme will have an impact on the building stock, which is expected to be used for decades by future users. Output 2.4 of PEEB Cool is dedicated to dissemination of PEEB Cool Programme results at the national and international levels. This ensures knowledge sharing and learning not only among PEEB Cool Subprojects but also with other programmes and initiatives in the sector, including through participation in international conferences, networking events, publications, webinars, and through partnerships with other organizations such as the World Bank, United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), Global Alliance for Buildings and Construction, NDC Partnership, World Green Building Council, World Resources Institute, and International Real Estate Federation). In other PEEB Cool Programme activities, knowledge sharing is also encouraged by providing a platform to share experiences for the local actors involved in the Programme with results shared on social media and dedicated internet sites. The dissemination of knowledge will be further supported through partnerships with national alliances to build momentum and ensure a widespread replication of PEEB Cool activities and reach. Social media (such as PEEB social media presence (twitter, Twitter etc.) will be adapted and given to reflect this scaling up.

## Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

Through the PEEB Cool Enabling Facility's activities, incomplete regulatory frameworks, implementation capacities for operation of buildings, and lack of awareness among stakeholders are identified as barriers that need to be addressed at national levels – with long-lasting impacts on the paradigm in construction beyond the duration of the Programme. The PEEB Cool Subprojects will address these issues by reducing costs for energy efficiency projects, the risks and barriers around deployment of low-emission and climate-resilient solutions in the buildings and construction sector are directly targeted.

## Contribution to the development of enabling policies

The PEEB Cool Enabling Facility explicitly addresses the need to introduce, enforce, and raise the ambition of regulations and policies to decarbonise the building sector and improve its adaptive capacity. Thanks to longstanding GIZ experience in this field, the PEEB Cool Subprojects will contribute to the successful embedding of the Enabling Facility's activities in established bilateral cooperation projects. PEEB Cool will complement existing efforts by providing a platform for dialogue between stakeholders.

This policy level work will complement construction Subprojects financed within the Investment

Facility funding for the demonstration of the feasibility of technical ambition rising, application of building energy efficiency labels, etc.

Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation and mitigation policies and strategies. In addition, the performance of all financed Subprojects will be studied under future climate conditions using climate models. This will enable achievement of the aim to construct buildings which will still be comfortable in the 2050s and beyond. The Programme will contribute to this objective by supporting the updating and development of national regulations, minimum standards or other commitments that ensure that new buildings are constructed with improved thermal comfort and resilience at a lower cost than increasing the cost base. The models showing the future improved thermal comfort and resilience of building users as well as the experiences with technical and regulatory changes will be transferred among countries to support their own climate-resilient development pathways, including activities essential to de-risk investment in better building design and better building technologies.

D.3 Sustainable development (max: 500 words, spread over approximately 1 page)

The construction of cool, energy-efficient buildings has wider benefits for health, the environment, especially in tropical and subtropical countries, and is a key element of climate-sensitive development. In addition to SDG 13 Climate Action the Programme will contribute to the following SDGs:

* **3 Good health and well-being**
    * For example, Indicator 3.4: Mortality rate attributed to cardiovascular disease or chronic respiratory diseases. High indoor temperatures exacerbate these diseases. Constructing buildings with improved thermal comfort could contribute to achieving country targets for this indicator.
* **5 Gender equality**
    * The Programme’s gender action plan includes activities that aim to increase the number of women benefiting from gender sensitive housing, commercial or social infrastructure and the number of women gaining access to long-term formal employment.
* **7 Affordable and clean energy**
    * For example, the Programme will support the installation of rooftop solar PV on buildings, thus contributing to access to affordable and clean energy.
* **8 Decent work and economic growth**
    * By supporting the construction of buildings that are also workplaces (administrative, commercial, industrial or education facilities), the Programme will contribute to decent work and increased productivity.
* **9 Industry, innovation and infrastructure**
    * For example, Indicator 9.1 – Develop quality, reliable, sustainable and resilient infrastructure and indicator 9.4 – Upgrade infrastructure and retrofit industries to make them sustainable, with increased resource-use efficiency and the adoption of clean technologies and processes


---
**Page 64**
---

The Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 62

The Programme will support actors in the construction value chain to improve their processes, contributing to the increased penetration of clean technologies and processes in the industry.

- **11 Sustainable cities and communities**
- The Programme will support the construction of public buildings such as schools and medical facilities as well as social housing. Ensuring access to affordable housing and basic services will strengthen the resilience of urban areas.

The specific sustainable development impacts of each Subproject under the PEEB Cost Programme will depend on the local circumstances (climate, country type of project). Below we describe the observed co-benefits of cool, energy efficient buildings.


### Environmental co-benefits

- Air quality will be improved as a result of the reduced demand for grid electricity due to the supported Subprojects. This will reduce the emission of other pollutants to the air as well as CO2.
- Air and water soil quality will be improved due to a reduction in pollution as a result of better sorting and disposal of waste, and the use of rainwater harvesting systems.
- Buildings will also be designed to use less water and for a reduction in light pollution through better design of exterior lighting, a reduction of noise pollution, and improvement of indoor air quality.

We will select some Subprojects in PEEB Cool which will have an environmental certification (HQE, EDGE, etc.) as well as a national standard to maximise the environmental co-benefits.


### Social co-benefits

While quantitative data is scarce, especially in developing countries, the health impact of high indoor temperatures includes increased heat stress and heat illness, headaches, dizziness, sleep disturbance and an increase in respiratory illnesses. As most people spend the vast majority of their time indoors, this has a significant effect on their quality of life.

In addition to the direct effects on individuals, there are also indirect effects such as increased spending on health care. Thermal comfort in hospital buildings in particular is essential. Studies have shown that patients who feel more comfortable recover faster and require less medication.


Benefits to education are hard to quantify. However, many studies reinforce the point that high classroom temperatures yield undesirable impacts, both on academic performance and perception of comfort.

In environments where ventilation is higher, temperatures are cooler and classrooms are more comfortable, there is a greater sense of educational satisfaction.

In the long run, these benefits mean that PEEB Cool's work at the public policy and Subproject levels improves equity of living conditions, a major aspect of social justice.


### Economic co-benefits

Economic co-benefits include poverty alleviation due to a reduction in energy bills for the users of buildings. For example, energy consumption for cooling of social housing in Tunis in 2050 will be 50%


---
**Page 65**
---

Higher in a business-as-usual housing unit than in a well-designed housing unit, with a corresponding saving in energy costs for residents.

It is expected that the Programme will create 27,000 jobs primarily in construction activities. PEEB Cook and others (2021) estimate that pandemic-related job losses could be offset by impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic. 26 So a large share of the value added in the construction sector is generated domestically; policies for low carbon buildings can also contribute to creating future-proof jobs in the sector. The Programme will support the development of new skills and knowledge, including the use of local materials or the re-use of building materials, through development of building renovation, and repair and maintenance skills. This will be particularly important where there is a shift away from conventional buildings. The Programme will be a catalyst for the development of a building-oriented economy involving local actors and less dependent on imported materials and know-how.

Comfortable office and indoor working environments are essential to employ well-being and health, overall productivity, and performance. A comfortable environment leads to improved performance and enhancement of incomes. A more comfortable environment promotes productivity and reduces absenteeism. The Programme will support the development of new skills in this area, including reduced ventilation rate cost less than 1% of the wages of an office worker. When we consider that the benefits of a comfortable working environment include reducing symptoms syndrome, reduced sick leave, the incremental cost of ventilation was significantly less than the benefits.

• Gender-sensitive development impact

The Programme targets a sector where women are under-represented. The Programme will support local and national governments to develop policies and strategies for promoting gender equality in entrepreneurship amongst others. Further details are provided in annex 8, gender assessment and action plan.


D.4 Needs of recipient users (see 500 words, approximately 1 page)

As the effects of climate change become more visible and disruptive, all the target countries face the great risk of temperature increases. The literature, shines a spotlight on the infrastructure in these countries that currently is not fit for purpose. Inefficient cooling and heating systems and/or ineffective building design means that in future buildings will see higher indoor temperatures than they do today. This is particularly the case with regard to residential housing and working environments with limited cooling – areas targeted within the Programme. The impact of climate change on urban heat islands is also a concern, especially for large cities; more people are at risk from the impacts of climate change combined with buildings that are unfit or purpose.

As most people spend the majority of their time indoors, ensuring building environments are at a comfortable temperature allows for thermal comfort to be regulated. In turn, this can limit the risks of health impacts associated with high indoor temperatures (dizziness, headaches, loss of concentration, especially in children). The Programme will support the development of new skills and knowledge amongst trainers and enhance learning and attainment for children in classrooms.

In densely populated cities, hotter temperatures and the heat island effect pose a dual threat. Mexico City, for example, has experienced an increase in average temperature of 6 degrees Celsius under a high emissions scenario. Considering studies have already shown that dwelling in Mexico with


---
**Page 66**
---

infrastructure deficiencies can be 5 degrees hotter than ambient temperature, future heat could become unbearable.

Vulnerability and exposure of beneficiary groups to climate change risks and impacts:

The Programme will invest in Subprojects addressing climate change impacts on low-income families (related to water scarcity), the elderly (related to housing and transport), children (related to education) and sick people (related to healthcare centres and hospitals). In many countries in the Programme, the population is very young, meaning that many new education facilities need to be built. This is particularly true for Sub-Saharan African countries, as illustrated for example by the population pyramid for Nigeria below.

| Figure 10 Age profile in Nigeria (2017) |
| --- |
| Population of Nigeria (2017) |

The socio-economic resilience of all social strata of the targeted communities will be strengthened as a result of PEES Cool investments, but the focus will be on low- and middle-income groups. Improving energy efficiency in buildings provides a wide range of benefits, including reduced energy consumption, improved comfort and employment, less energy subsidies, higher property values, etc. The approaches used by low-income groups also address social inclusion issues such as adequate housing and better quality educational and health facilities.

Potential of the proposed Programme or project to strengthen institutional and implementation capacity

In many countries there are no effective energy efficiency building codes that would help enforce requirements for energy-efficient buildings. Developing and enforcing energy efficient building codes and other related standards is important to set requirements for private investment. Such public policies are developed within the Programme, based on the lessons learned from demonstration projects in Sub-Saharan Africa. The development of such public policies will be further complemented by capacity building measures to ensure sound application of said public policies for the countries involved.

Subproject level, the Programme provides excellent opportunities to deliver information and training to local stakeholders, especially the departments in the sectorial ministries (e.g. health, education) that are in charge of developing and enforcing energy efficiency building codes. Many of these institutions are interested in these approaches but have little to no knowledge of the concepts of energy efficiency and how they can be implemented. This is a great progress potential.

Absence of alternative source of financing

Despite the importance of improving energy efficiency in buildings in the fight against climate change, the financial resources available for this purpose remain limited. The total amount spent on energy efficiency investments amounted to around 250 billion USD in 2018 whereas 1,090 billion USD would be needed each year between 2016-2050 to meet the objective of the Paris Agreement of staying below an increase of 2°C.

40 IRENA, 2019

While previous investment amounts had not been reached in the past, the COVID-19 crisis has led to a dramatic worsening of public finance. Moreover, in many of the PEEB Cool countries, “factors such as the stability of local currencies and the creditworthiness of national financial institutions can be critical in the availability of financing.” Political instability, corruption, and weak governance are also barriers to accessing international capital. Inequalities, equity, social inclusion, and limited capacity for research and development, technical innovation, and educational initiatives are other constraints on government access to finance.

The IFC report in this report, Building a resilient financial and policy blueprint for emerging markets, states that “Many emerging markets face the urgent need to meet the shortfall in affordable housing.” The priority has been placed on building fast and at scale, while the attention on green construction has been limited. This is despite the fact that green buildings can be more energy efficient and have a longer lifespan with margins of conventional practices. Development finance institutions can help demonstrate the viability of alternative financing through their own investments in partnership with private lenders for the market to follow.”

### Country ownership, including existing strategies and policies on energy in the building sector and adaptation, is described in more detail for each country in the respective country form.

The Programme development has involved consultations with stakeholders, which are detailed in Annex 1. These consultations have been informed by the experience of PEEB programme. For this reason, some consultations have also taken place with potential Subproject owners, such as in Indonesia. These potential Subproject owners include banks, health facilities and other public sector institutions that are part of government programmes.

AFD will involve NDAs during the implementation phase of PEEB Coop by providing information on activities carried out and Subprojects financed.

All countries covered by the PEEB Cool Programme have a national strategy or plan for energy efficiency in buildings as an integral element of their NDCs. Many countries on this list have included specific commitments to GHG mitigation in the building sector in their NDCs.

Some countries of PEEB Cool, such as Mexico, Nigeria and Indonesia, participate in the IGACI multi-country project, which is a joint initiative between the World Bank and AFD. The project focuses on the replacement of cooling systems with high performance, the elimination of HFC refrigerants, and the energy performance labelling of equipment.

Many countries have implemented renewable energy, mostly through decentralised PV systems and mini grids. The labelling of energy performance for cooling systems and home appliances is often part of a national action plan. Among the counties of PEEB Cool, only twice or are we aware of such labelling being in place.

We have found very few countries with a national policy to develop local materials or low carbon materials.

One of the unexpected outcomes of the ongoing PEEB programme has been the extent of country ownership resulting from synergies between the project and policy level activities, and how this supports sustaining the country ownership post-programme. In many PEEB countries, there are programmes in place for energy efficiency in buildings that include public sector institutions such as housing units, schools and healthcare centres, which involve various stakeholders including government agencies, NGOs, private or public multilateral funds.

In Tunisia, for example, GIZ has supported the extension of a hospital, as well as the construction of another hospital. Both of them integrate energy efficiency measures discussed with local actors. In addition, GIZ has prepared a guide for energy efficiency in health facilities that will enable other hospitals to implement similar measures. This is an example of how PEEB Cool can provide energy efficiency solutions not only in those two projects but also in the other projects planned for the next few years, such as the extension of another hospital and the construction of a new public hospital, meaning that the impact of PEEB's support will extend well beyond the project itself.

In short, since the Programme team works on both policy and project level activities, this enables the identification of opportunities where policy level support can build on the momentum created by successful investment projects to create a lasting impact on certain sectors.

## D.E. Efficiency and effectiveness (max.: 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Cost-effectiveness and efficiency regarding financial and non-financial aspects

The financial model has assessed the annual debt service for a Subproject owner of a business-as-usual (BAU) scenario compared to the annual debt service for a Subproject owner of a green building (GCF financed) scenario. The BAU was compared to the annual debt service of the same Subproject taking into account EE measures financed by GCF and AFD ("green building scenario"). The question in the analysis was whether the additional cost of financing EE measures was compensated by GCF concessionality, compared to BAU, and to which extent.

During Programme implementation, the share of GCF co-financing will be calculated in order to cover the increased costs. This financial structure will address the financial barrier at investment while providing the minimum concessionality.

The Programme is designed to address efficiency issues that various bottlenecks restraining EE investments in buildings are caused by. The use of concessional loans will encourage the Subproject owners to engage into greener buildings.

By developing a local offer as detailed in section B.3 the Programme will not crowd out private investment but instead pave the way by creating an enabling environment so that private sector can take lead at the end of the life of the Programme and ensure sustainability of the impacts.

## Programme financial viability and other financial indicators

Economic Impact Ratio (EIR) has been assessed in Annex 2 to taking into account financial savings, GHG avoidance and improved comfort of buildings. In total, EIR for the Programme is assessed at 48.5%. This is strongly influenced by the estimated positive impact per direct beneficiary (estimated annual energy saving per household of 100 kWh) and the average cost of energy efficiency investments. This ratio is well above the GCF benchmark.

Several Subprojects were assessed representatives of the main types of Subprojects outlined out in the Programme document. These Subprojects are representative of a wide range of projects with different profiles of energy efficiency savings and climate change types (mitigation/adaptation), and low or middle-income Subproject owner. Specific data sets were constructed on the basis of the inputs provided by AFD and Programme partners.

With GCF blending, the incremental debt service costs for green buildings are reduced to a value that stakeholders in the targeted countries are willing and able to invest at the current nascent stage of the market for green financing.

In all cases, the involvement of GCF finance makes an important difference. The over cost triggered by the EE measures in the annual debt service is considerably offset, when there is GCF concessionally - mainly through a reduction in interest rate - and/or grant component to support the incremental energy efficiency debt service in all cases where the goal is climate change mitigation.

The level of concessionality of GCF used in the model very depending on the country (climate scenario), type of Subproject, and the level of concessionality from the donor country (less developed countries (LDC) or not), and takes into consideration some cases where the level of concessionality from AFD Group is already high. The level of share of GCF financing shall be higher in some cases to maximize the impact on climate change mitigation. The methodology for calculation of the degree of concessionality is described in the section 4.3.1 on the Feasibility Study.

## Co-financing, co-leveraging ratio and mobilised long-term investments

The co-financing ratio is calculated based on GCF, AfD Group and German Government financing amounts.

(i) GCF financial contribution: €175,000,000 EUR and €45,000,000 USD
(ii) Total co-financing contributions: €83,500,000 EUR and €281,000,000 USD
(iii) GCF cofinancing ratio = 17%, on the EUR side and 4% on the USD part

The leverage ratio of the GCF is 6.9x for EUR and 35x for USD part.

The expected indirect long-term low-emission investment mobilised as a result of the implementation of Programme activities, notably in Component 2, is significant, as the public policies supported will deliver major impacts on the Programme’s target markets and contribute to the development of new models or practices.

Because of the expertise and capacity building that accompany the financing projects and the public policies being implemented, know-how and expertise will be acquired in energy efficiency by the local actors of the sector building, which will ensure the construction of high-quality low-emission buildings in the future. The Programme will also provide training to developers and other stakeholders, including developers to help them promote energy efficiency measures in their future investments. Coupled with the investment frameworks to be developed in key related sectors, market and therefore business and investment opportunities can be identified to set the scene for mobilising long-term investments independent of the Programme.

## Application of best practices

The Funding Proposal has been prepared by the PEEB secretariat, which works under the umbrella of the Global Alliance for Buildings and Construction (Global ABC). Being a member of the Global ABC steering committee, PEED is fully aware of the latest developments in the building sectors.

Component 2 is based on the vast experience of GIZ in supporting partner governments in their energy transition efforts. The module, which this proposal will be based on, is based on the best available information and practices in OECD countries and in international development cooperation.

## E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### At Investment Facility level
During the disbursement period, AFD or PROPARCO will receive implementation reports from Subproject owners to track progress and identify potential areas for performance improvement opportunities.
In this document, Subproject owners will report at least on an annual basis on performance indicators (ICD) eq avoided for a period of 1 year.

### Task team leaders
Based in AFD or PROPARCO's Paris headquarters will visit Subprojects and meet Subproject owners regularly. For example, these missions take place on average once a year, resulting in a Subproject implementation report being shared with all the Subproject stakeholders (Subproject owner, borrower, investor, local government, etc.). These visits are also an opportunity to gather feedback from Subproject owners and request remote interactions with the borrowers or investors. The task team leaders will be responsible for overseeing Subproject implementation and for approving funding disbursements.
AFD Group's monitoring process foresees an annual internal quality review process.

### Monitoring of dissemination occasions
PEES Cool training and enabling capacity experiences are disseminated globally at least on an additional 20 occasions, picked by their relevance.


---
**Page 84**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 82

E

As per AFD’s procedures and depending on the disbursement modalities selected at the Subproject level, controls will be performed before every disbursement made by either AFD Group staff or an independent third party.

At the end of the disbursement period, AFD will prepare a Subproject completion report summarizing the main events of the implementation phase, the results achieved, and the lessons learned. In addition, about a year after the drafting of the completion report, the Evaluation department of AFD may conduct an ex-post evaluation of Subprojects or appoint a local consultant to do so. The consultant will be tasked with evaluating the impact of the Subproject on its intended beneficiaries and the environment. All evaluations are peer-reviewed by independent experts and checked for credibility and robustness, and methodological revisions to ensure they are replicable (in-line with GCF evaluation principles).

It is worth noting that the evaluation function within AFD refers to the same evaluation principles and criteria developed by the OECD Development Assistance Committee (DAC) and its evaluation protocols are similar. AFD adheres to its principles for evaluation, which are impartiality and independence. The objective of these principles is to ensure that evaluations are credible, relevant, and useful in informing policy decisions.

Enabling Facility level

The Enabling Facility will monitor and report its progress through established monitoring systems and a dedicated monitoring and reporting focal point within the policy implementation team. Country activities will be closely coordinated between country staff and headquarters coordinators in monthly coordination meetings and bi-annual Enabling Facility steering meetings. This allows for a close follow-up of progress.

Through the policy team reports submitted to the PMU, the AE will be able to give direct input into the reporting activities of the AE, in addition to legally agreed upon technical and financial reporting procedures between AFD and GIZ.

GIZ’s projects are internally randomly selected for independent external evaluation. Financial audits take place at yearly intervals. OECD DAC criteria are a core element in these evaluations.

At Programme level

An annual performance report (APR) will be submitted to the GCF following reporting requirements under the AMA and the FAA. The APR will be submitted to the Secretariat for the period ending on 31 December within 60 days after the end of the relevant period. The first APR will be

submitted following the end of the calendar year in which the Parties enter into the relevant FAA and the last APR will be submitted within six months of the end of the relevant reporting period. The implementation reporting period of the Programme will start from the date of effectiveness of the FAA until the Programme implementation end date.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 2 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1
**Category:** Technical and operational  
**Probability:** Medium  
**Impact:** Medium  

Description: Insufficient availabilities and capacities among stakeholders at subproject and policy levels hinder or endanger Programme progress.

Mitigation Measures:
- At policy level, policy technical assistance will work towards better coordination through clear and adaptive communication with all involved parties and carry out capacity reinforcement activities to improve the overall effectiveness of EE measures.
- At Subproject level, subproject technical assistance will be selected on an experience and technical capability basis in order of merit design. PEEB Cool will assist Subproject owners to ensure that EE measures are properly implemented.

### Selected Risk Factor 2
**Category:** Other  
**Probability:** Low  
**Impact:** Medium  

Description: The political priorities of partner countries are not aligned with PEEB Cool objectives in terms of energy efficiency targets or climate change goals.

Mitigation Measures:
- Partner countries selection will be thorough and focus on those with higher potential to be chosen. PEEB Cool countries are characterized by the fact that sectors with high energy efficiency or adaptation potential (education, health) are key priorities for those countries.
- In addition, PEEB Cool will work with the private sector, which, to a certain extent, evolves independently from public strings.

### Selected Risk Factor 3
**Category:** Credit  
**Probability:** Low  
**Impact:** Low  

Description: A counterpart is not able to reimburse PEEB Cool loans.


---
**Page 87**
---

# Mitigation Measures

At Subproject level, AFD Group is already mitigating this risk through thorough analysis of counterpart credit risk. This is achieved through deep knowledge of public and private counterparties and regular internal rating assessments. If needed, AFD can use a sovereign guarantee to mitigate this risk.

Credit risk of the portfolio overhang is mitigated by diversifying across sector (e.g., subproject type, etc.). Maximum PEEB Cool amounts per country will catalyse geographical diversification even more.


## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Forex | Low | Low |

### Description
A borrower is not able to service the loan reimbursement in case of a local currency devaluation.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
AFD Group is usually lend in EUR or USD. If AFD or PROPARCO identifies this risk at appraisal level, it can advise the counterpart to mitigate this risk through foreign exchange hedge.


## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Reputational | Low | Low |

### Description
Construction activities supported by PEEB Cool directly or indirectly result in (forced) displacement of a population.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Subprojects that bear too high environmental and social risks (project type A) will not be eligible to PEEB Cool financing. In case of planned forced displacement, the ESMP that will be elaborated and PEEB Cool will propose a framework to mitigate this risk.


## Selected Risk Factor 6

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| MLFT | Low | Medium |

### Description
PEEB Cool activities contribute to money laundering or financing of terrorism.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
AFD and PROPARCO appraisal processes involve MLFT diligences. In addition, AFD, PROPARCO and GIZ staff perform a mandatory annual MLFT training that allow them to keep in mind the stakes of this risk.


## Selected Risk Factor 7

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sanctions | Low | Medium |


---
**Page 88**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 86

## Description

| Counterparts that are part of PEEB Cool are subject to international sanctions. |
| --- |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Appraisal and disbursement processes include verification with EU, France and World Bank exclusion lists that exclude any business with listed counterparts. Portfolio approach allows to focus on those counterparties where such activities are possible.

## Selected Risk Factor 8

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description

Contractual obligations related to AFD Group and/or GCF financing instruments are too restrictive (cost/benefit analysis from teams and/or Subproject owners).

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The Programme will hire a PMU to support Subproject owners in following their contractual obligations, such as reporting.

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

In line with the World Bank Group E&S Framework, the risks and impacts taken into account by AFD in its projects are those defined for PEEB. For the PEEB Cool Programme, the E&S Standards and ESRM of AFD, PROPARCO and GIZ will apply.

### Eligibility criteria as stated in the Feasibility Study exclude Subprojects with a risk level A.

For minimal, moderate and substantial risk Subprojects (C, B and R), international standards, World Bank standards, and national regulations apply. However, as regulations in the countries where AFD operates are sometimes not aligned with international standards, the ESG team will consider additional guidance, group policies and directives produced by international standard-setting organisations with more than 70 years of experience in the energy sector development.

The Project-level Environmental and Social Management Framework is provided in Annex Ga. The ESFM sets out the principles, rules, guidelines and procedures to assess the environmental and social risks and impacts of the Subprojects dedicated for the PEEB Cool Programme presents the framework which guides projects of ESMIF, ESMAF and GIZ. A full ESIA and ESMP including a stakeholder engagement plan will be produced for all future projects.

The institutional-level Grievance redress mechanism is presented in the Annex 6a, section 4.2 & Grievance Redress Mechanism.

In terms of E&S risk management, AFD Group applies World Bank Group Standards, including the World Bank E&S Framework* and IFC Performance Standards and the related Guidance and Interpretation Notes.

The Reference E&S Standards for the PEEB Cool Programme will be national legislation and IFC Performance Standards and related Guidance Notes.

The E&S risk management approach for the Programme follows the IFC Interpretation Note on Financial Intermediaries** and is in line with the Equator Principles***.

The applicability of IFC Performance Standards for the PEEB COOL Programme is summarised in the ESMP.

Under the Programme, a Subproject will be understood as:

- **Direct financial subproject:** loans or grants to finance and support investments with potential to improve energy efficiency and energy efficiency measures in buildings or within the construction ecosystem. The Implementing Entities correspond to public entities, civil society organisations or private companies.
  
- **Intermediated subprojects:** the partnership between AFD or PROPARCO and a Financial intermediary (FI), meaning a credit facility. The credit facilities will then be divided into several loans

*<https://www.worldbank.org/en/topic/projects-objectives/environmental-and-social-framework>

***<https://www.equator-principles.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/06/Equator-Principles-6.pdf>
****<https://publications.iadb.org/sites/default/files/interpolation_note_6.pdf>
*****<https://inquirer.principles.at>

## Policy projects are technical assistance delivered within PEEB Cool under Component 2 of the Programme (Enabling Facility).

With regards to intermediated Subprojects, the EAS due diligence is integrated by AFD Group into the review and the monitoring of the Subproject as a whole around four main steps:

- **Subproject identification** based on the level of EAS risk of the Subproject.
- **Review of the Fit for Sustainability (FFS) Environmental Social Management Framework (ESMF).**
- **If gaps are identified with AFD’s requirement and the FFS ESMF, definition of an environmental and social management plan (ESMP) to strengthen the FFS ESMF.**
- **EAS monitoring.**

The ESMF describes the Programme approach to EAS risk management in each of the above cases. For each case, specific risk management principles that will apply depend on the implementing entity (AFD, PROPARCO, GIZ) involved.

An indicative list of EAS risks and impact per sector eligible for the Programme is detailed below:

Increase of noise pollution. Impacts on existing infrastructure and networks. Increased exposure to health and safety risks. Environmental impacts for projects and local government, stakeholders, environment, local communities. |

Environmental impacts for projects and local government, stakeholders, environment, local communities. |
