## Industry
- Improvement of energy or water consumption within the construction ecosystem (cement plant, steel production, LCA in industrial sectors [textile, etc.])
- Improved management practices (re-use and recycling of materials), waste-to-energy activities

## Transportation
- Greenfield or brownfield project of rail or urban mass transit, bus station

## Other building sector
- Energy efficiency in market, shopping mall, offices, ecosystem innovation support, etc.

At Subproject and End-beneficiary project level, grievance mechanisms must be designed to receive and facilitate the resolution of complaints from affected communities, vulnerable populations, groups and individuals. The need for relevance of such mechanisms shall be assessed during Subproject appraisal phase and EAS evaluation. Grievance mechanisms must be tailored to the level of Subproject EAS risks and impacts, with a focus on ensuring that they are accessible, transparent, and effective. Such mechanisms should be responsive process consistent with Subproject and/or local context. The mechanism shall be free, and shall not impede access to justice or other remedies for affected communities, vulnerable populations, groups and individuals of such mechanisms and inform Subproject stakeholders accordingly. Grievance mechanisms at Subproject and End-beneficiary project level are developed firstly in order to allow amicable resolution of a dispute or concern. If this is not possible, the matter should be referred to be solved through an appropriate legal process.

Stakeholder consultations are conducted at all agency and subproject scales. They are described as in the forms and in Annex 7.

In the framework of the Programme and line with GCF approach to stakeholder engagement, AFD requires implementing entities to ensure the effective engagement of communities, vulnerable populations, groups and individuals affected by or potentially affected by Subprojects and End-beneficiary projects. This is done in compliance with the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, and shall be disclosed on AFD’s website. This includes the GCF EAS Framework, which is made available in the languages of the partner countries.

All necessary measures to ensure that activities financed by the GCF are adopted and implemented in such a way as not to undermine any provisions of the GEF AEI Policy, as well as with the AE and EE policies will be taken. SEAH safeguarding also apply to all Subprojects and End-beneficiary projects, including direct and indirect financial Subprojects.

## Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
**Page 92**
---

The PEEB Cool programme will seek to develop women’s roles as consumers, employees, and entrepreneurs in the green building sector, improving their access to bioclimatic housing and their economic participation in the sector.

### Key challenges faced by women:

- Due to existing gender inequalities, women often participate to a limited extent or are excluded from decision-making processes concerning buildings at all levels (households, local communities, sectors).
- Women have difficulty accessing finance and training to learn the trade and skills required in the building sector.
- Unbalanced power relations between men and women (including spending decisions on buildings)
- Social norms that reduce women’s access to education, gainful employment and economic resources
- Increased vulnerability of women in accessing decent housing
- Limited access to land and property rights, especially for women in rural areas and informal property
- Issue accessing credit to own or rent houses
- Differentiated access to services due to gendered spatial design of housing
- Negative attitudes or perceptions of women employed in companies

The PEEB Cool Programme targets a variety of sectors. AFD has developed Sectoral gender toolkits (SGT) which promote a crosscutting approach and provide a resource for technical experts and task team leaders. These SGTs are designed to help the programme design gender-sensitive, responsive and transformative measures to integrate in the Subproject activities. PEEB Cool Subprojects can belong to the following sectors:

- Housing and urban development (mainly affordable housing programmes)
- Education (schools, universities, classrooms in rural areas, science complex, training centres)
- Health (hospitals, clinics)
- Public buildings and other building sector (public buildings, markets, shopping malls, offices)
- Industry (improvement of energy consumption or water consumption in industrial processes)
- Transport (mainly public transport)

For each of those sectors, the gender assessment annexed provides a summary of the main gender issues, as well as good practices for gender integration.

Regarding intermediate financial measures, AFD generates an initial diagnosis of the situation of the financial institution and prepares EASs (compliance with the standards of the WB Group, assessment of professional equality internally and in the product approach etc.). During the appraisal process, AFD elaborates a financing plan that includes the identification of the sources of funding for the project and its financing arrangement. The content of these EAS action plans and the gender measures is designed in relation to the initial diagnosis / gaps identified in regards to AFD’s expectations, the FPs ambitions for progress, the various financial instruments available.

Specific commitments govern the implementation of the EAS action plan and of measures on gender by the partner FI during the implementation of the subproject, as well as the reporting methods (EAS + gender report). The GIZ will provide technical assistance to the partner FI in preparing these documents and the reporting through the technical assistance financial line part of the global financing of the subproject.

In addition to the annexed gender assessment and action plan, at the level of the Enabling Facility, the gender mainstreaming concepts of GIZ will be applied during implementation. This includes at least a gender sensitivity analysis in all project phases (feasibility study, design, implementation) when no relevant sectoral analysis is available within the GIZ country portfolio. These analyses are conducted in a combination of local stakeholder consultations and legal and regulatory framework review and aim to identify


---
**Page 93**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 91

The ways in which the Enabling Facility’s intervention may be used to work towards gender equality. Based on the results of a gender action plan for each country will be supplemented with gender-related activities and points of attention for the Enabling Facility's activities.

## Impact Statement
* Increase access of vulnerable communities, including women and girls, to the negative impacts of climate change, and increased involvement and access of women in sustainable building, efficient cooling system and improvement of industrial processes subprojects and their benefits.

## Outcome statement
The Programme will enhance and support gender mainstreaming within the financed Subprojects. It will participate in increasing the awareness and building the capacity of the Implementing Entities at national, regional and local level. The Programme will promote and supervise the equal participation and access to all genders in the Subprojects for women and girls.

## Output statement
The Gender Approach for the Investment Facility in the Programme is Subproject-specific. Specific indicators and activities are therefore defined on a case-by-case basis according to the framework established in the GCF Gender Strategy.

Considering the objectives and sectors targeted by the Programme, gender-related activities can be addressed through:

### Cross-cutting approaches to gender equality
* **Gender-based analysis of climate change exposure:** 100% of the Subprojects will have integrated a gender analysis.
* **Capacity building of Subproject owners on gender related topics:** 100% of the IEs involved in the Programme have built capacity on gender.

### Specific thematic approaches: 100% of the Subprojects developed through the Programme include a gender equality objective through:
* Women’s economic empowerment: promotion of gender equality at work within IEs and their clients, women's empowerment and economic participation on End-beneficiary projects, support to women entrepreneurship, financial inclusion of women, etc.
* Equal access to services across all Subprojectbeneficiaries

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Financial management
The detailed provisions regarding financial management of the GCF resources by AFD will be described in the FAA. Reporting will be provided by AFD to the GCF specifying among others:
* The amounts already committed and disbursed by AFD Group to local partners by country and by Subproject.
* The amounts already committed and disbursed by GIZ by country;
* The remaining amount on different GCF accounts;
* A description of the financial management system for each rolling horizon; 
* For the loan component, a list of the incidents recorded during the repayment period of the loan provided to local partners.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 92

## Subsidiary agreements will be signed between AFD and PROPARCO and AFD and GIZ.

For non-sovereign borrowers, credit risk monitoring will be carried out by AFD through the assessment of the financial statement of the borrowers, monitoring of financial covenants, etc.

For due diligence for each financing (direct or intermediated) will be carried out by AFD's local agency staff or consultants. The GCF has a network of 17 national agencies in 17 countries around the world. There is a local agency involved for every country where the programme is implemented. Each subproject and end-beneficiary project will go through an AFD appraisal process cycle, which was reviewed by the GCF during the preparation of this proposal. For further details please see Annex V at ADF application to the GCF to become an accredited entity.

## At AFD level, disbursement will concern:

- Direct payments for project TA entities
- Loan disbursements on Subprojects, either (1) refinancing, 2) direct payment or 3) advance on subproject costs. The terms and conditions of these disbursements will be defined on a subproject-by-subproject basis and will be described in the financing agreement signed between AFD and the borrower.
- Funds transfer to GIZ and PROPARCO whose disbursement will be reported to AFD.

If AFD has to grant loans at fixed interest rates, it will do so only if there is no other option. In case of foreign exchange risk, AFD conducts hedging operations, also known as swaps, when granting loans at a fixed interest rate or in foreign currencies. In terms of interest rates, all assets and liabilities are economically equivalent. The swap transaction would be executed by the GCF's financial advisor. If the market moves suddenly, raising the cost of AFD's borrowing - on the liabilities side - the interest accrued on loans granted - on the assets side - would follow the same trajectory.

## Procurement

AFD's procurement rules are in line with international standards provided by the World Bank Group. The AFD procurement guidelines as detailed in Annex 19 will apply to public-owned local partners that are in charge of implementing the project. For private-owned local partners, local partners will be required to comply with AFD procurement rules for a detailed presentation of AFD procurement rules, see the Procurement Guidelines available on AFD's website.

For privately-owned Subprojects sponsors, PROPARCO will apply the procedures applicable to the specificities of the PROPARCO private sector Subprojects clients.

GIZ procurement and awarding rules apply for procurement within component 2 for which GIZ is the procuring agency.

AFD, PROPARCO and GIZ are accredited to the GCF; as such their procurement rules and procedures are assessed and evolutions are communicated on a yearly basis during the AEs' self-assessment exercise.

No confidential information. The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

    - Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) or
    - Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) or
    - Other relevant environmental and social management systems (Resettlement Policy Framework, Indigenous People’s Plan, Land Acquisition Plan, etc.)

- **Annex 8**: Gender assessment and project/programme-level action plan (template provided)
- **Annex 9**: Legal due diligence (regulation, taxation and insurance)
- **Annex 10**: Procurement plan (template provided)
- **Annex 11**: Monitoring and evaluation plan (template provided)

- **Annex 13**: Co-financing agreement, if applicable (template provided)

- **Annex 15**: Evidence of internal approval (template provided)
- **Annex 16**: Map(s) indicating the location of proposed interventions
- **Annex 17**: Multi-country project/programme information (template provided)

- **Annex 20**: First level AMCLIFT (KYC) assessment
- **Annex 21**: Operations manual (Operations and maintenance)
- **Annex 22**: Assessment of GHG emission reductions and their monitoring and reporting (for mitigation and adaptation projects)
- **Annex X**: Other references

**Annex 22 is mandatory for mitigation and cross-cutting projects.**

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP19

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths

- The proposed programme aims to scale up an existing programme, Programme for Energy Efficiency in Buildings (PEEB) Goal.
- PEEB is aiming to transform the construction industry towards a more resilient and energy-efficient building (so called green building), design, construction and operation of buildings.

### Points of caution

- The programme has a tentative multi-country project pipeline, which is to be fully developed during the preparation phase.
- Proper methodological underpinning with respect to the selection criteria and specific technical and financial thresholds, alongside detailed implementation plan.
- A clear methodology needs to be put in place to ensure the delivery of the proposed activities as indicated in the funding proposal. It is important that the accredited entity (AE) strictly adheres to this methodology.
- The programme sets a set of parameters that will be set forth in the term sheet through negotiation and hopefully approval process.

The components have two complementary roles: one enabling capacity, and another enabling facility. The components have been designed with the aim of addressing what are preventing the construction industry in Africa from adopting and implementing current unsustainable practices to a low-carbon, climate-resilient development pathway.


---
**Page 117**
---

The programme aims to support the goals of the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The programme also is in alignment with the GCF sector guidance on energy efficiency, building systems and energy efficiency.

### II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

#### 2.1 Programme background

- According to the International Energy Agency (IEA) direct and indirect emissions from buildings reached a record high of 7 GT in 2021, after having risen an average 1 per cent per year since 2013. While there was a 10 per cent drop in the level of CO2 emissions in 2020, largely due to lockdowns, slowing of economies, difficulties households and businesses faced in maintaining their energy supply, and reduced travel, the building sector's share of global final energy consumption accounted for 36 per cent of global final energy consumption and 17 per cent of energy-related CO2 emissions.
- In its report, IEA indicates that despite the expected rebound in emissions in 2021 being moderated by continued power sector decarbonization, buildings remain off track to achieve carbon neutrality by 2050. The IEA estimates that for new buildings, the share of zero-carbon building stock would need to be zero-carbon-ready as soon as 2030.
- It is projected that with rapid urbanisation and economic development, final energy demand from buildings is predicted to increase globally by 50 per cent by 2050 compared with 2015 under business-as-usual (BAU) scenarios. Energy intensity per square meter of the building stock would need to improve by 30 per cent by 2050 in order to meet Paris climate goals (UNEP, 2017).
- The Programme for Energy Efficiency in Buildings (PEEB) is a flagship project under PEEC fund, an expansion of the previous Energy Efficiency in Emerging Countries Fund (EEEC), which operated during COP21. The aim of PEEB is to transform the building sector by promoting sustainable technologies and energy efficiency practices through targeted support for energy efficiency in large-scale projects with technical assistance through policy advice.
- PEEB Goal takes the experiences and lessons learned from PEER. Specifically, PEEB Goal targets the following:
  - Clean cooling as a majority of the countries with developing and emerging economies are located in hot climates, both dry and humid;


---
**Page 118**
---

### Sustainable building construction materials that make the most out of locally sourced materials and construction techniques;
- **Private sector involvement**, which must be spearheaded by the national public authorities.

#### Key barriers to be addressed by PEEB Goal are the following:
##### Technical barriers:
1. Project stakeholders lack technical knowledge and experience in planning and implementing energy efficient and resilient building projects based on holistic design principles and efficient cooling technologies.
##### Financing barriers:
2. Building owners are reluctant to invest in the higher upfront cost of energy efficient, low carbon and resilient buildings, especially with the economic crisis caused by the COVID-19 pandemic;
3. Due to lack of experience with such projects, banks are reluctant to expand their portfolios into this sector. Tails of financial instruments missing and/or too restrictive to be widely used;
4. Industry actors are reluctant to adapt their services for the construction and operation of energy efficient and resilient buildings, specifically in times of economic crisis; and
##### Institutional and regulatory barriers:
5. National strategies and policies for the decarbonization of the sector often lack clarity (e.g., lack of clear structural targets and specific actions);
6. Building codes lack specific requirements for energy efficiency in buildings and related infrastructure, which is mandatory for most types of buildings only, or still under discussion;
7. Sector stakeholders lack knowledge and awareness on energy efficient and resilient building solutions; and
8. National governments are highly fragmented.

#### PEEB Goal aims to target 11 countries across 4 continents. The countries are selected based on the below criteria:
- Vulnerability due to increased temperatures and climate extremes like heat waves;
- A preliminary gap assessment for specific needs in the residential, healthcare and commercial sectors;
- Similarity of technical barriers; and
- The general relevance and experience of the AE.

#### PEEB Goal countries cut across six different climate zones: equatorial, tropical, desert or arid, humid subtropical, Mediterranean, and continental. The diversity of selected countries is expected to provide a wide range of lessons for other countries in the region. The projects will address both mitigation and adaptation impacts, with strong benefits of socio-economic resilience of the communities involved. These benefits are likely to be reflected in improved access to public services such as education and health. The pipeline of projects and sub-projects (here sub-projects refer to those that are not yet funded but have been identified for potential co-financing) will cut across seven priority sectors: residential, hospital, education, office, retail, transport and hotel, identified based on country-specific vulnerability.


---

---

## (a) Out of the five countries previously supported by PEEB, three countries are included in PEBB Cool. While policy support for these three countries will be provided through PEBB Cool to expand support provided by PEEB, the project pipelines of PEEB and PEEB Cool in these countries remain separate.

### (a) Mexico: PEEB support was provided on technical guidance and building regulation on energy efficiency in buildings. PEEB Cool will provide support for energy efficiency in the hotel sector. PEEB Cool will provide support on energy efficiency in housing for vulnerable populations, with a possibility to further provide institutional support on energy efficiency.

### (b) Morocco: PEEB support was provided to identify and develop energy performance improvement plans for buildings and appliances. PEEB Cool will provide support on energy efficient buildings and appliances. It also supported enforcement of building codes and facilitated discussions and capacity-building. PEEB Cool will provide financial support to unlock the potential of energy efficiency in buildings, including through co-financing with PEEB; and

### (c) Tunisia: PEEB support was provided through technical assistance in health facilities, including awareness raising activities, building stock and energy demand analysis as well as high environmental quality (HEQ) certification. Financing support was provided to two hospitals for the installation of solar water heaters. In addition, a market study and identification of energy-efficient social housing projects was undertaken. PEEB Cool will provide financial support to unlock the potential of energy efficiency in buildings.

### (d) The total programme funding amount is EUR 1,012.5 million and USD 326.2 million, of which GCF funding is requested for EUR 131 million and USD 45 million in senior loans and EUR 4 million in co-financing. In addition, the programme will provide financial support to co-finance senior loans and EUR 8 million in grants. On top of all co-financing, EUR 2.5 million is co-financed by the Federal Republic of Germany in the form of grant.

### (e) The programme's environmental and social safeguards (ESS) risk is categorized B.


## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Investment Facility (total cost: EUR 987.9 million and USD 326.2 million; GCF cost: EUR 534.0 million)

### Under component 1, the Investment Facility is to be established to provide necessary financing to implement sustainable building practices through projects. This is achieved through (i) technical assistance at design phase to review plans and make recommendations;


---
**Page 120**
---

### (ii) provision of finance (including concessional loans) to address the financial barrier linked to high investment costs and related debt services indexed by green buildings, and (iii) technical assistance at construction phase to monitor the proper implementation of bioclimatic design and energy efficiency measures and ensure the targeted climate impacts of the programme are achieved.

15. At the design phase of projects, technical assistance will be provided to review and identify potential cost-effective solutions that can be provided to improve project financial and economic feasibility or pre-feasibility studies. This support will be provided under activity 1.1.3., while being essential to ensure that the following climate impact targets for each project type will be achieved:

- (a) Mitigation targets for a renovation project: 40 per cent GHG emissions reduction or 40 per cent energy consumption savings and/or 20 per cent cost savings.
- (b) Mitigation targets for a new construction project (2 out of the following targets): 20 per cent GHG emissions reductions and/or 20 per cent energy consumption savings and/or 20 per cent cost savings.

16. The Investment Facility will also provide technical assistance to all projects that will receive concessional loans, including those provided through a public or private entity provided with potential additional financing sources and self-financing from project owners; or (b) intermediated financing to a financial intermediary. The project pipeline will be developed in close collaboration with the relevant national authorities. These criteria are set out in the funding proposal and the term sheet. The determination of the GEF level of concessional support is based on the amount calculated to over the incremental costs associated with adoption of green building technologies.

#### Green building ecosystem actor

#### Component 2: Enabling Facility (Total cost: EUR 71.5 million; GCF cost: EUR 19 million)

The Enabling Facility will support the adoption of policies and regulations to perpetuate and solidify the gains made in the building sector, as well as capacity-building and enforcement are built. Support will be provided in (i) develop sector investment frameworks or national energy efficiency strategies, (ii) establish a regulatory framework, and expand policy frameworks to mainstream energy efficiency in the building sector, including establishment/improvement/enforcement of building certification, building code or energy efficiency standards, and other relevant policies, (iii) raise awareness and technical understanding on bioclimatic building design, building energy performance assessment, and energy-efficient technologies. The Programme will share knowledge and experience demonstrated by the Programme utilizing regional and global platforms.

#### Project management (Total cost: EUR 812 million; GCF cost: 2.2 million)

This component will support the executing entities (EEs) and project implementing entities (PIEs) in preparing, implementing, and monitoring the investment activities, in compliance with the contractual obligations that are included or referred to in the legal agreements entered into with the AE. The GCF portion of the project management cost is less than 2 per cent of the grant portion of requested funding from GCF and in compliance with the GCF policy on fees.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential

**Score: High**

PEER Cost has a mitigation impact potential of 1,562-799 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MCO2eq) avoided for the project lifetime. The impact is estimated based on the emission factors of electricity for each country and a single gas emission factor for all countries; and the baseline scenario is defined as the business-as-usual scenario in which countries will directly benefit from the programme. The programme envisages annual energy savings of 23,107 GWh per year at the end of the project’s lifetime, in addition to enhancing the resilience of 35,869 physical assets in total.

Through the Investment Facility financing for 11 projects is envisaged, and through the Enabling Facility, 4 investment frameworks/initiatives and 24 policy proposals are to be developed and prepared. The programme also estimates 27,000 jobs created in green building construction jobs, of which 20 per cent are targeted to be occupied by females.


---
**Page 122**
---

28. The underpinnings of the envisaged impact potential of the proposed programme are supported by most contemporary urban sector research on improving efficiency of building stocks. The McKinsey Center for Business and Environment and C40 Cities (2017) study emphasizes that in cities with a population greater than 5 million, energy savings from buildings can be as high as 30-37 per cent of the carbon reductions needed to reach the 2030 objective of a 1.5°C world. According to the study, the largest potential for energy efficiency lies in large and medium-sized semi-dense/middle-income cities. Among building efficiency actions, ultra-high-efficiency new buildings stand with the greatest potential impact, followed by HVAC and energy efficient water heating, as well as lighting and building upgrades.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential.
**Scale: High**

25. The programme breaks down traditional financial, institutional and technical barriers through an integrated approach that has been undertaken through PEEB, which was initiated in 2018 in five countries. PEEB Cool is a scale-up programme of PEEB aiming to contribute to unlocking climate change mitigation potential by providing cities with the necessary tools and resources for scaling up energy efficiency in buildings by creating an enabling environment and contribution to regulatory framework and policies.

26. The programme applies innovative sustainable green building solutions, incorporating bioclimatic design, passive cooling systems, solar shading, rainwater harvesting, and waste heat production. PEEB Cool enables strong technology transfer where the 11 countries demonstrate examples of best practices in energy efficiency in buildings. Knowledge sharing and dissemination will support the sharing of these experiences through regional and global platforms.

27. Assuming that 50-75 per cent of new buildings (built in 2017 and later) are constructed to ultra-high-efficiency standards, developing world cities (including the 11 countries targeted by the programme) can use this source to achieve 23 per cent of the 1.5°C emissions target by 2030 in large, low-income cities; 42 per cent in mega cities and 10 per cent in large, semi-dense/middle-income cities.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential
**Scale: High**

28. The programme has a high potential to generate environmental benefits through reduced air pollution and improvement of waste and wastewater disposal through bioclimatic design and rainwater harvesting systems. It also contributes to economic benefits, including increase in productivity, reduced mortality and heat stress, and improved access to public services by reducing energy costs for cooling. PEEB Cool will create 105,000 green jobs and economic opportunities with market creation and 27,000 green jobs as a programme co-benefit. A strategic approach is being adopted in each country to ensure that the benefits are felt in buildings for each country to promote participation of women in the decision-making process, as well as other underrepresented groups.

29. PEEB Cool will provide benefits over multiple SDGs including SDG 3 (good health and well-being), SDG 4 (gender equality), SDG7 (affordable and clean energy), SDG8 (decent work and economic growth), SDG10 (reduced inequality), SDG11 (sustainable cities and communities) and SDG12.

30. The programme's sustainability is also ensured by building envelope retrofit strategies such as solar shading, window glazing, air tightness and insulation all of these measures can


---
**Page 123**
---

3.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

PEER-Coal will promote and accelerate investments in projects addressing climate change impacts for vulnerable populations, including low-income families (residential housing), children and elderly people (health care facilities), and workers exposed to heat stress due to high indoor temperatures. As noted above, the programme has pre-identified the sectors that are prioritized by each country based on the vulnerabilities identified.

While the potential impact of energy efficiency measures in buildings in this sector is going to be significant, ranging from 60 to 100 per cent globally by 2050. The 2020 HIA report emphasized that the most effective way to achieve these impacts would be through policy signals to drive a range of measures, including passive building design, material efficiency, low-carbon materials, efficient building envelope measures, and highly efficient lighting and appliances. These measures are expected to have a significant impact on energy demand in new buildings and existing buildings. According to the baseline analysis conducted by the AIE on the energy efficiency of building codes for thermal regulation (T65) in the 11 countries:

* **9 out of 11 countries have T65 in force:**
    * 1 country has not yet introduced T65
    * All countries have TRs or TR projects; and
    * The Asian countries involved in PEER-Coal all have TRs

The proposed programme is offering a tangible opportunity for building energy codes and related policies to be strengthened, modernized, and harmonized across the building sector in 11 targeted countries. Building energy codes are regulatory instruments that specify minimum energy efficiency performance requirements for buildings. They typically either mandate certain energy-efficiency characteristics for buildings or provide guidance aligned with technology performance and provide target energy use levels for a building as a whole. Building energy codes can be mandatory or voluntary and are often complemented by additional energy efficiency building initiatives.

3.5 Country ownership

**Scale: High**

PEER-Coal is aligned with the countries' nationally determined contributions (NDCs), the Montreal Protocol and its Kigali Amendment, and countries’ strategies.

* **Albania:** The programme is in line with the NDC and national legal regulations and plans, including the National Energy Efficiency Action Plan (NEEAP) (Law No. 125/2015), Albanian Law on Energy Performance of Buildings (Law No. 139/2016), and the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS). The programme will provide support to implement energy efficiency policies and measures to further enhance implementation, adoption of policy reforms and larger investments. Albania is committed to the Kigali Cooling Efficiency Programme (K-CEP).

* **El Salvador and Georgia:** 2017.

* **IBA: 2020. Global IBA Regional Roadmap for Buildings and Construction 2020-2050.


---
**Page 124**
---

**(a) Argentina:** The country’s NDC aims to achieve reduction in GHG emissions by 15 per cent by 2030 through efforts such as diesel replacement by natural gas, nuclear power and biofuels, energy efficiency promotion at all levels, disseminated renewable energy production and consumption, and the development of a national carbon market. This target is in line with the electricity regulatory framework and housing policy and will provide support to existing programmes. The country is committed to the K-CEP and is also a Cool Coalition partner.

**(b) Costa Rica:** The country NDC includes an unconditional 44 per cent GHG reduction target by 2030, conditional on international support for financing and technology transfer, achieving partial carbon neutrality by 2021 and becoming a net carbon-neutral economy by 2050. HFCs are not included in the target. The country will provide support to contribute to the country’s ambition for a net zero emission economy. Costa Rica is committed to the Kigali Programme and is also a Cool Coalition partner.

**(c) Ethiopia:** The country NDC includes unconditional targets of 35 per cent reduction, including Law No. 42/1994/76m, Law No. 88/1998 (A), Law No. 90/2001 and 15/2007, as of 2015, and contributes to Djibouti Vision 2035. Djibouti is a Cool Coalition partner.

**(d) Indonesia:** The country’s NDC includes a conditional target of reducing energy sector GHG emissions by 41 per cent below the business-as-usual (BAU) level in 2030, or 67 per cent relative to BAU level. The programme will provide support to achieve the Government’s energy sector targets and compliance and enforcement of Minimum Energy Performance Standards.

**(e) Mexico:** The country adopted the General Climate Change Law in 2012, and aims to accomplish a reduction of GHG emissions by 30 per cent below its 2005 level by 2024. It has set a baseline and by 36 per cent with international support for its NDC. The country has been implementing the Montreal Protocol since 1991 and has established an energy conservation programme, which includes energy efficiency standards, regulations on waste management, water management, responsible and sustainable procurement, energy audits, and energy performance contracts. Mexico is committed to the K-CEP and is also a Cool Coalition partner.

**(f) Morocco:** The country’s NDC includes actions in relation to energy efficiency in the building sector with 30 per cent GHG reduction targets. PEER Goals aim to support contribution to the country's energy efficiency laws and National Energy Strategy, as well as a national strategy for sustainable buildings and construction. Morocco is also a Cool Coalition partner.

**(g) Nigeria:** The country’s NDC includes a conditional target to reduce GHG emissions by 45 per cent below its BAU level in 2030, or 71 per cent annual improvement in energy efficiency (30 per cent by 2030), and 13 GW off-grid solar power plant. The programme will contribute to the country’s National Energy Efficiency Action Plan and National Building Code. Nigeria is committed to the Kigali Programme and is a Cool Coalition partner.

**(h) North Macedonia:** The country's NDC includes the building and transport sectors, which have the dominant share of reaching the GHG emissions reduction target. The programme will contribute to the implementation of the National Strategy for Energy Development until 2050. Several national energy efficiency and climate plans are under development in the country. The country has set a baseline and targets need to be set. The country ratified the Kigali Amendment in 2020 and is also a Cool Coalition partner.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

GCF funding will deliver mitigation benefits at EUR/USD 49.27 per tCO₂eq (total GCF project costs/expected lifetime emission reductions) and adaptation benefits at EUR/USD 195 per hectare/year (total GCF project costs/expected lifetime adaptation benefits). The average cost of financing ratio is 1.5.

The EIR carried out economic and financial analysis, including sensitivity analysis. The EIRR for the programme is assessed as 48.5%. The economic value added of the Programme largely is due to its contribution to GCF emission reduction potential and positive impacts for health and well-being of the funded activities.


### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

Programme background: PEEB Goal aims at enhancing energy efficiency in the building sector in the target countries through an investment facility to support public and private investments in energy-efficient buildings. The Programme is designed as an enabling facility to develop a sectoral investment framework, elaborate policy proposals and build capacity for implementation of the proposed measures.

The programme includes the improvement of air quality as a result of the reduced demand for grid electricity, which has positive impacts on health, especially in respect to improved water and soil quality due better waste management of construction and operation waste from buildings. Improved designs are also expected to result in reduced water requirement, light and energy use, and lower emissions of greenhouse gases. The Programme includes improved overall health, quality of life and living conditions of building occupants.

### Environmental and social risks (ESR) assessment

The ESR assessment has classified environmental and social (E&S) categories of the programme to potentially have "substantial" E&S risks (category B+) as per the AEF's risk categorisation, which would be equivalent to a high risk category in GCF and AFSD risk categorisation. The Programme is not in this category and that it is within the AEF’s E&S risk accreditation level. All subjects with risk category A as per GCF and AFSD categorisation (high risk) will be excluded from the

### Compliance with GCF ESS standards:
The paragraphs below provide a brief description of the programme’s compliance to ESS standards.

#### ESS 1: Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts
This section outlines how the programme will assess, manage, mitigate, and monitor at the project level (both public and private projects) within their pipeline of building subprojects in the target countries. The EMSP will develop a comprehensive environmental and social management plan for each subproject under the programme and how these will be assessed, managed, mitigated, and monitored at the project level. The ESS performance of the programme as well as that of its subprojects will be evaluated by the implementing entity against various criteria such as extent and size of potential negative impacts; importance of the construction phase; temporality of the impact; government’s commitment to manage the subproject; and the presence of different geographical areas, among others. The EMSP will assist each subproject owner in defining the E&S framework for their projects, including developing an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), formulating the subproject's E&S commitments in an Environment and Social Commitment Plan (ESCP), and conducting a pre-construction audit of the subproject. The EMSP will also conduct an E& S audit for existing facilities and developing the management plans of its subprojects.

#### ESS 2: Labour Working Conditions
Potential risks and impacts of the programme related to labour working conditions include occupational health and safety risks of the workforce, including those associated with construction activities and operation phase activities of the subprojects. This is associated with occupational health and safety risks of the workforce, including those associated with construction activities and operation phase activities of the subprojects. This is associated with occupational health and safety risks of the workforce, including those associated with construction activities and operation phase activities of the subprojects.

#### ESS 3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention
The activities under the programme will potentially generate waterborne and hazardous materials. In addition, during construction and operation phases, there may be potential for noise pollution, dust generation, particulate matter, dust, solid and liquid waste, including potential for fuel spillage from vehicles, as well as other environmental impacts such as soil erosion, sedimentation, excavations and concreting may also be expected. Potential conflict with the use of local natural resources is another risk that could arise during construction activities. To mitigate these risks and impacts, the programme intends to ensure that subproject owners will acquire the appropriate environmental permits and comply with all relevant national laws and regulations, including requirements. Management and mitigation plans and other instruments will likewise be developed in accordance with international best practices, globally accepted environment, health, and safety guidelines and good international industry practice.

#### ESS 4: Community Health, Safety and Security
The local communities where the subprojects are located may also face potential risks during construction activities and operation phase of these activities. These may include potential exposure to nuisance noise and dust particularly during construction activities, exposure to physical and chemical hazards, increased potential for traffic accidents due to movements of heavy machineries and vehicles, as


---
**Page 127**
---

well as reduced amenity because of diminished access to housing and/or public utility infrastructure. Possible conflicts between construction workers, security personnel and the local population may also occur. Nevertheless, these risks and impacts will be assessed in detail during design and implementation phases. The programme’s activities will include the establishment of mitigation measures with regard to the possible impacts generated by the programme. These measures will aim at preventing or mitigating any potential conflicts and tensions that may occur through preparation of a workers’ code of conduct as well as development of an emergency preparedness and response plan.

### ES 59: Environmental Management

In the framework of the programme, all subprojects shall be designed to avoid involuntary resettlement considering all alternative options. The environmental impact assessment (EIA) will be conducted in accordance with IFC’s requirements will be followed. To minimize displacements and adverse social and economic impacts from land acquisition or restrictions on land use, compensation will be provided for loss of assets and livelihoods. In addition, a resettlement plan will be prepared in consultation with appropriate disclosure of information, consultation, and the informed participation of those affected. A detailed resettlement plan will be developed as part of the EIA. The Resettlement Action Plan will be required and shall be carried out following the ESRS requirements. An indicative terms of reference for a resettlement consultant will be provided in the Environmental Restoration Plan and a Resettlement Policy Framework are attached in the ESMP.

### ES 60: Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources

The programme’s activities, particularly during construction, may adversely affect natural habitats and avoid activities that may extensive land clearing. The programme will also implement measures to minimize adverse impacts on biodiversity and ensure that there is no cover change in the locations where the construction and refurbishment activities will be executed. Should waste disposal practices not comply with IFC’s requirements, significant loss of habitat and biodiversity may occur due to inappropriate disposal of solid waste and discharge of wastewater.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES 7: Indigenous Peoples

The project is being carried out in a number of countries where indigenous peoples are present and as a result has triggered the Indigenous Peoples Policy (IPP) and the associated Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) of the IFC. The IPP recognises that indigenous peoples, as social groups with identities that are distinct from mainstream groups in national societies, are among the most marginalized and vulnerable populations in many countries. The programme will ensure that there is no cover change for indigenous peoples to participate in and benefit from project-related activities. In accordance with the IPP, the programme will develop an Indigenous Peoples Action Plan (IPAP) as developed an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework and an indicative outline of Indigenous Peoples Plan that will be submitted to IFC. The IPAP will provide a framework for ensuring benefits from the design and implementation of programme-related activities, and (b) benefit from the programme’s activities. In addition, the IPP requires that all projects involving indigenous peoples subprojects on indigenous peoples will be identified during the E&S risk identification process and where significant impacts are expected, an Indigenous Peoples Plan needs to be designed following the IPAP.

### ES 8: Cultural Heritage

The programme’s activities, particularly during construction, may adversely affect cultural heritage. The programme’s activities will ensure that these will be protected in the implementation of the subproject action plan and their preservation will be supported. Implementing entities will be required to ensure that, where a subproject involves the use of cultural heritage, there is no cover change for any significant benefits from the use of cultural heritage will be ensured through subproject design and execution phases. The programme’s activities will also ensure that there are no adverse impacts on cultural heritage and the ESMP has provisions for managing change finds through

### Implementation arrangements

Each subproject supported by the programme will be required to assign a primary safeguard specialist. Additional specialists (as required) may also be allocated as needed.

The AE will have overall responsibility for ensuring that all participating implementing entities from each country is expected to manage their supported portfolio of projects in accordance with the E&S Management Plan and any other relevant guidance during the preparation of subprojects. Capacity-building to improve the E&S capabilities of the implementing entities will also be explored by the AE. The implementing entities will be responsible for ensuring that all subproject activities are consistent with the E&S management plan, and compliance with the E&S management plans of third parties and subcontractors. ### Gender policy

The AF has conducted a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with all requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

The gender assessment provides an overview of possible gender issues related to the project. However, the quality of the analysis varies by country, with some countries still requiring further work on their gender assessments. The gender assessment covers areas such as access to land, credit, and finance; access to markets; and access to technology. It also includes a review of gender equality laws in each country (e.g., Botswana, Tanzania). This includes a review of gender aspects of each country's participation in the project. The gender action plan is based on the findings from the Gender Assessment Against Women), constitution, legal system, access to employment and access to resources. It also takes into account specific subproject-level gender actions before the start of the implementation of PEBB projects. The subject-based gender assessments will be the bases for developing subproject-level, context-specific gender action plans. The analysis and development of the gender action plan are expected to engage various stakeholders at both the subproject level and country level.

The current overall analysis indicates the existence of enabling environment to engage in gender equality initiatives. However, some countries present more advanced states than others, including Mexico, Argentina, and Costa Rica, where they take a proactive approach towards women's empowerment and inclusion and increase the participation of women in the sector. General data for the construction sector is sparse, but there are some examples of countries that have taken steps committed to promoting women's engagement and inclusion in the construction industry, with current women's participation levels ranging from less than 7% up to 14 per cent. Some countries have introduced gender equality policies or initiatives aimed at increasing women's participation in the sector. Activities that promote women's engagement and inclusion include implementing a variety of measures such as providing training for women, establishing quotas, organizational policies; prioritizing gender equality when crafting national policies; collecting sex-disaggregated data on women's participation in the construction industry; and ensuring that women are represented in decision-making levels, and strengthening workplace protections for women. For example, Argentina has introduced a law requiring all companies to have at least one woman on their board of directors. The Chamber of Construction Industry to promote the inclusion of women in the sector. This has resulted in an increase of women's participation from 4 to 14 per cent. Argentina is another example where the government has taken steps to support legislation and practices to promote gender equality. Women are better represented in the service sector, particularly in areas such as administration, secretarial work, clerical, engineering, and accounting-related jobs. Some of the barriers identified for women to engage in construction include a lack of access to education and training, stereotyping the industry, uncomfortable working environment and limited access to information related to energy.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The GCF is requested to provide a loan of EUR€131 million and USD$24 million in addition to a grant of EUR€50 million for the construction of new buildings in 11 countries over 4 continents. AFD group will co-finance a sector loan of EUR€85 million and USD$291 million, and AFD will provide a grant of EUR€5 million. The government of Germany is co-financing a EUR€3 million loan.

The programme consists of two facilities: an investment facility (EUR€97.8 million and USD$24.6 million) and a grant facility (EUR€10.5 million). For the most part, the sub-project pipeline in each country will be determined by national implementation plans.

The programme is designed to provide great flexibility. The programme allows for the provision of co-financing from AFD or other multilateral development banks (MDBs) and/or directly made by AFD or intermediated through the local financial partners (LFPs). No more than 90% of the total maximum amount of the GCF Sub-participation will be disclosed to public project ownership. The split amount between direct and indirect financing is unknown at this stage.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity's capability to execute the current programme (low risk)


---
**Page 131**

## Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

### Credit risks: GCF will assume the credit risk of borrowers in direct financing.

- Credit risks: GCF will assume the credit risk of LPFs. LPFs will assume the credit risk at the end borrower level, there is no minimum credit rating in LPF's internal eligibility criteria.
- For public entities, AIF may be provided as two separate tranches. GCF may be drawn by the fact that the credit risk will be shared between LPFs and the AIF. The GCF may also provide a facility for limiting the risk of default of the LPFs and counterparts in its appraisal procedure. The AIF may be used to mitigate the risk of over-indebtedness of the country.
- To diversify risk and avoid a concentration risk, there is a cap of 25% per single entity borrower. In addition, no more than 20% of GCF loan will be provided to a single LPF in case of intermediate financing.

### Co-financing risks: Other projects with intermediate financing or project finance in GCF portfolio are subject to co-financing risk. The co-financing risk is shared between the main borrower/sponsor, in this programme, the contribution from sub-basin recipients and LPFs will be divided by LPFs and the AIF as committed in the lending agreement, not co-financing.
- There is sizable financing in AIF Group which will be covered by GCF. The AE will have a certain flexibility to determine the ratio between GCF and AIF group tranche to avoid over-indebtedness of the country. A 1:6 ratio needs to be met for each country level to mitigate the co-financing risk.

### Completes implementation arrangement: The programme is at large scale covering multiple countries, a complex implementation arrangement may pose a risk of delays in the programme.
- Due to the great flexibility, most of the decisions will be made downstream. In case of LPFs, the decision will largely lie with the public counter party. The quality of the loan portfolio will be a key factor for GCF's ability to provide financing and LPF's ability for direct financing and LPF's ability for intermediate financing to ensure and monitor that the loans are in line with the environmental and social performance criteria such as a minimum performance target for mitigation and adaptation.
- These targets are different from the ones set by the AIF Group. The AE will be the enabling facility and investment facilities has to be well coordinated. The AIF country office will have an influence in implementing projects in participating countries.

### FX risk: If the loans are denominated in hard currencies (USD and EUR), the volatility of the local currencies could potentially affect return impact on the borrower's ability to repay.
- The provision of two currencized facilities is recommended. It is recommended that each currency can be provided separately. If the GCF loan approved by the board become insufficient to complete the funded activities resulting from the exchange rate movement, the LPF may provide a facility for supporting the project financing.


---
**Page 132**
---

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

#### Compliance risk (medium risk)
- None of the beneficiary countries involved in this project (i.e., Albania, Argentina, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Ethiopia, Georgia, Morocco, Nigeria, South Macedonia, Sri Lanka, Tunisia, and Vietnam) are currently subject to any United Nations Security Council (UNSC) resolutions (UNSCRs).
- AFD, as AE, confirmed that no individual or entity listed on any UNSC sanctions list, including the United Nations Consolidated Sanctions List, will be involved in any manner with the project.
- AFD confirmed that during project appraisals and before implementation, AFD would require financial entities to screen any entity/individual against UNSC Resolutions. Ex ante annual risk assessments will be conducted by the financial entity in cooperation with the beneficiary; they will verify if the entity follows this process.

#### Only counterparty for which AFD must complete due diligence is Deutsche Genossenschaftsbank (DGB). The AFD has put in place a comprehensive framework for ensuring compliance with the applicable laws and regulations regarding money laundering and terrorist financing (ML/TF). These policies are set out in circulars that are widely disseminated throughout the institution.

AFD provides training on AML/CFT to all its employees, including those in its network branches. AFD also has a dedicated team responsible for monitoring compliance with these policies and existing clients to ensure compliance with AML/CFT policies. AFD does not receive deposits from the public or accept cash payments. Instead, it only accepts electronic transfers of financial information. AFD meets the requirements of basic fiduciary standards for AML/CFT.

AFD set up an updated internal AML/CFT procedure using a risk-based approach in accordance with its revised policies and procedures on anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing (AML/CFT) and its revised credit management system, and it has put out various procedures for implementing its revised policy on financial sanctions. The AFD also has a dedicated team responsible for monitoring compliance with these policies and existing clients to ensure compliance with AML/CFT policies.

AFD confirms that the due diligence efforts aim to identify and verify the identity of counterparties and their beneficial owners before beginning business relationships (i.e. KYC). Due diligence is performed by the employee in charge of the relationship, who will be systematically subjected to second-level controls performed by the Operational Risk Monitoring and Control Team. The level of control depends on the degree of risk that the counterparty represents with regards to ML/TF as established in the AFD AML/CFT internal procedures. Additional points of control are added in the presence of aggravating factors:

(a) the presence of a politically exposed person;
(b) the presence of negative elements (including reputation); and/or
(c) the presence of a counterparty incorporated in a country that has been blacklisted by the Financial Action Task Force (FATF) or other international organization on ML/TF.

AFD also requires counterparties and relevant stakeholders to submit their latest audited financial statements. The AFD will systematically identify them. In case of ML/TF suspicions or if AFD has not been able to identify beneficial ownership, AFD will proceed to submit a suspicious transactions report to the French Financial Intelligence Unit (UOFF).

During project appraisal and throughout the project's life cycle, AFD provides its employees with training on financial sanctions and anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing (AML/CFT) policies adapted by France, the EU, the United States, the United Kingdom and the United Nations. Such screening is also included in the processing chain for payments issued by the AFD financial department. The purpose of such screening is to ensure that no transaction is made with a person who is a current or former government official or winner of a call for tender funded by AFD, under financial sanction or operates in sectors under embargo (e.g., Iran, Syria) and/or is located in countries subject to sanctions by the United Kingdom.

AFD confirmed that after a project is completed, procedures for reimbursing and winding up equity investments are monitored carefully because they could reveal fraudulent practices.
